\psi_{L}(\ext_{u,v})=\psi_{L}(\ext_{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}})=f
. Because
f
is
idempotent, we have that

\psi_{L}(\ext_{u^{\mathopen{}\left|v^{\prime}\right|\mathclose{}},v^{\mathopen{}\left|v^{\prime}\right|\mathclose{}}})=\psi_{L}(\ext_{u^{\prime\mathopen{}\left|v\right|\mathclose{}},v^{\prime\mathopen{}\left|v\right|\mathclose{}}})=f
,
thus

\psi_{L}(\ext_{x_{g}u^{\mathopen{}\left|v^{\prime}\right|\mathclose{}}x_{h},y_{h}v^{\mathopen{}\left|v^{\prime}\right|\mathclose{}}y_{g}})=\psi_{L}(\ext_{x_{g}u^{\prime\mathopen{}\left|v\right|\mathclose{}}x_{h},y_{h}v^{\prime\mathopen{}\left|v\right|\mathclose{}}y_{g}})=e
.
Therefore, because of length-synchronicity of

\mathcal{R}_{e}
, it follows
that

\displaystyle\frac{\mathopen{}\left|x_{g}u^{\mathopen{}\left|v^{\prime}\right|\mathclose{}}x_{h}\right|\mathclose{}}{\mathopen{}\left|y_{h}v^{\mathopen{}\left|v^{\prime}\right|\mathclose{}}y_{g}\right|\mathclose{}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathopen{}\left|x_{g}u^{\prime\mathopen{}\left|v\right|\mathclose{}}x_{h}\right|\mathclose{}}{\mathopen{}\left|y_{h}v^{\prime\mathopen{}\left|v\right|\mathclose{}}y_{g}\right|\mathclose{}}

\displaystyle\frac{\mathopen{}\left|x_{g}\right|\mathclose{}+\mathopen{}\left|v^{\prime}\right|\mathclose{}\cdot\mathopen{}\left|u\right|\mathclose{}+\mathopen{}\left|x_{h}\right|\mathclose{}}{\mathopen{}\left|y_{h}\right|\mathclose{}+\mathopen{}\left|v^{\prime}\right|\mathclose{}\cdot\mathopen{}\left|v\right|\mathclose{}+\mathopen{}\left|y_{g}\right|\mathclose{}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathopen{}\left|x_{g}\right|\mathclose{}+\mathopen{}\left|v\right|\mathclose{}\cdot\mathopen{}\left|u^{\prime}\right|\mathclose{}+\mathopen{}\left|x_{h}\right|\mathclose{}}{\mathopen{}\left|y_{h}\right|\mathclose{}+\mathopen{}\left|v\right|\mathclose{}\cdot\mathopen{}\left|v^{\prime}\right|\mathclose{}+\mathopen{}\left|y_{g}\right|\mathclose{}}

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\left|v^{\prime}\right|\mathclose{}\cdot\mathopen{}\left|u\right|\mathclose{}

\displaystyle=\mathopen{}\left|v\right|\mathclose{}\cdot\mathopen{}\left|u^{\prime}\right|\mathclose{}

\displaystyle\frac{\mathopen{}\left|u\right|\mathclose{}}{\mathopen{}\left|v\right|\mathclose{}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathopen{}\left|u^{\prime}\right|\mathclose{}}{\mathopen{}\left|v^{\prime}\right|\mathclose{}}\,\,\text{.}

So we can conclude that

\mathcal{R}_{f}=\{(u,v)\in\mathsf{Con}(\Sigma)\mid\psi_{L}(\ext_{u,v})=f\}

is length-synchronous.
Thus, by the second point of
Proposition
5.10
we may assume that
the evaluation language
\mathcal{E}_{\psi_{L},e}
is regular and its
syntactic morphism is quasi-aperiodic for all
\varphi_{L}(L)
-reaching

e\in O_{L}
with
\mathcal{R}_{e}
length-synchronous.
This implies again, by
[
28
, Theorem VI.4.1]
, that for
each
\varphi_{L}(L)
-reaching
e\in O_{L}
with
\mathcal{R}_{e}

length-synchronous, there exists an

\mathsf{FO}_{\Gamma_{\psi_{L}}}[<,\mathsf{MOD}]
-sentence
\nu_{\psi_{L},e}
defining

\mathcal{E}_{\psi_{L},e}
.

For proving
L\leq_{\mathrm{cd}}\biguplus_{e\in\mathcal{Z}}M_{e}
we must thus make use of the
oracles to
\biguplus_{e\in\mathcal{Z}}M_{e}
.
All of the following predicates can be computed by a circuit family of
constant depth and polynomial size with access to these oracles.
More concretely, by accessing oracles to
\biguplus_{e\in\mathcal{Z}}M_{e}
, for all

e\in\mathcal{Z}
we may assume that we have a predicate
\pi_{e}^{\mathsf{exact}}

such that for all
w\in\Sigma^{+}
and

i,i^{\prime},j^{\prime},j\in[1,\mathopen{}\left|w\right|\mathclose{}],i\leq i^{\prime}<j^{\prime}\leq j
the following
holds:

\displaystyle w\models\pi_{e}^{\mathsf{exact}}(i,i^{\prime},j^{\prime},j)

\displaystyle\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad

\displaystyle w_{i}\cdots w_{i^{\prime}}w_{j^{\prime}}\cdots w_{j}\in\Sigma^{\triangle},e\circ\psi_{L}(\ext_{w_{i}\cdots w_{i^{\prime}},w_{j^{\prime}}\cdots w_{j}})=e\text{\lx@nobreakspace and }

(4)

\displaystyle\Delta(w_{i}\cdots w_{i^{\prime}})>0

For all
\varphi_{L}(L)
-reaching
e\in O_{L}
that are
not in
\mathcal{Z}
we
may assume, by Lemma
5.13
, that we have
the
\mathsf{FO}[\mathsf{+}]
-definable (and hence constant-depth computable) predicate

\pi_{e}
at hand. It has the following properties: for all
w\in\Sigma^{+}

and
i,i^{\prime},j^{\prime},j\in[1,\mathopen{}\left|w\right|\mathclose{}],i\leq i^{\prime}<j^{\prime}\leq j
:

•

if
w\models\pi_{e}(i,i^{\prime},j^{\prime},j)
, then

w_{i}\cdots w_{i^{\prime}}w_{j^{\prime}}\cdots w_{j}\in\Sigma^{\triangle}
and

•

if
w_{i}\cdots w_{i^{\prime}}w_{j^{\prime}}\cdots w_{j}\in\Sigma^{\triangle}
,

\Delta(w_{i}\cdots w_{i^{\prime}})>0
and

e\circ\psi_{L}(\ext_{w_{i}\cdots w_{i^{\prime}},w_{j^{\prime}}\cdots w_{j}})=e
,
then
w\models\pi_{e}(i,i^{\prime},j^{\prime},j)
.

We can first build an approximate matching
\mu
relative to
L
. This is
done totally analogously as done in Section
5.3.4
by
replacing the there appearing
\pi_{e}
for each
e\in\mathcal{Z}
by our predicate

\pi_{e}^{\mathsf{exact}}
: indeed, Remark
5.15
states that the
predicates
\pi_{e}
from of Lemma
5.13

could have been replaced by the predicate
\pi_{e}^{\mathsf{exact}}
.

Thus, as in the proof of
Proposition
5.16
we may assume
that we have full access to the matching relation
M^{\Delta}(w)
of our input
word
w
.

For verifying if a given word
w\in\Sigma^{\triangle}
is in
L
we follow the
same approach as the main construction in Section
5.3.5
.
It is however important to stress that this time we cannot assume
quasi-aperiodicity of the syntactic morphisms of the evaluation languages

\mathcal{E}_{\psi_{L},e}
for all
\varphi_{L}(L)
-reaching
e\in O_{L}
.
Still, we build formulas

•

\overline{\eta}_{d,r}^{\uparrow}(x,y)
for all
d\in[0,d_{L}]

and all
\varphi_{L}(L)
-reaching
r\in R_{L}
and

•

\overline{\eta}_{d,r}(x,y)
for all
d\in[0,d_{L}]
and all

\varphi_{L}(L)
-reaching
r\in R_{L}

that will have the following properties (as
\eta_{d,r}
and

\eta_{d,r}^{\uparrow}
) for all
w\in\Sigma^{\triangle}
and all

i,j\in[1,\mathopen{}\left|w\right|\mathclose{}]
:

•

if
i
is matched to
j
in
w
, then

(w,M^{\triangle}\left(w\right))\models\overline{\eta}_{d,r}^{\uparrow}(i,j)

if, and only if,
\nd(w_{i}\cdots w_{j})\leq d
and

\varphi_{L}(w_{i}\cdots w_{j})=r
;

•

if
w_{i}\cdots w_{j}\in\Sigma^{\triangle}
, then

(w,M^{\triangle}\left(w\right))\models\overline{\eta}_{d,r}(i,j)
if, and
only if,
\nd(w_{i}\cdots w_{j})\leq d
and

\varphi_{L}(w_{i}\cdots w_{j})=r
.

It remains to define the formulas
\overline{\eta}_{d,r}
and

\overline{\eta}_{d,r}^{\uparrow}
for all
d\in[0,d_{L}]
and all

\varphi_{L}(L)
-reaching
r\in R_{L}
.
For the definition of the
\overline{\eta}_{0,r}^{\uparrow}
and the

\overline{\eta}_{0,r}
we can simply reuse
\eta_{0,r}
and

\eta_{0,r}^{\uparrow}
as in the proof of
Proposition
5.16
respectively
(
\eta_{0,r}
will make use of our sentence
\nu_{\varphi_{L},r}
).
So let us assume
d>0
.

We first construct for all
\varphi_{L}(L)
-reaching
e\in O_{L}
a formula

\overline{\chi}_{d,e}(x,x^{\prime},y^{\prime},y)
such that for all

w\in\Sigma^{\triangle}
and

i,i^{\prime},j^{\prime},j\in[1,\mathopen{}\left|w\right|\mathclose{}],i\leq i^{\prime}<j^{\prime}\leq j
we have that if

w_{i}\cdots w_{i^{\prime}}w_{j^{\prime}}\cdots w_{j}\in\Sigma^{\triangle}
and
i^{\prime}
is
matched to
j^{\prime}
in
w
, then given

\ext_{w_{i}\cdots w_{i^{\prime}},w_{j^{\prime}}\cdots w_{j}}=\ext_{x_{1},y_{1}}\circ\ext_{a_{1},b_{1}}\circ\cdots\circ\ext_{x_{k},y_{k}}\circ\ext_{a_{k},b_{k}}

the stair factorization of
\ext_{w_{i}\cdots w_{i^{\prime}},w_{j^{\prime}}\cdots w_{j}}

provided by Lemma
3.6
, we have

(w,M^{\triangle}\left(w\right))\models\overline{\chi}_{d,e}(i,i^{\prime},j^{\prime},j)
if, and only
if,
\nd(x_{\ell}),\nd(y_{\ell})<d
for all
\ell\in[1,k]
and

\psi_{L}(\ext_{w_{i}\cdots w_{i^{\prime}},w_{j^{\prime}}\cdots w_{j}})=e
.
Given
w,i,i^{\prime},j^{\prime},j
and the associated stair factorization as above,
note that if
\psi_{L}(\ext_{w_{i}\cdots w_{i^{\prime}},w_{j^{\prime}}\cdots w_{j}})=e
, as
e

is
\varphi_{L}(L)
-reaching, then
\varphi_{L}(x_{\ell})
and

\varphi_{L}(y_{\ell})
are
\varphi_{L}(L)
-reaching for all
\ell\in[1,k]
.
If additionally
\nd(x_{\ell}),\nd(y_{\ell})<d
for all
\ell\in[1,k]
, we
can inductively make use of the formulas

\{\overline{\eta}_{d-1,r^{\prime}}\mid r^{\prime}\in R_{L}\text{ $\varphi_{L}(L)$-reaching}\}

in order to evaluate

\varphi_{L}(x_{1}),\varphi_{L}(y_{1}),\ldots,\varphi_{L}(x_{k}),\varphi_{L}(y_{k})
.

As expected, the problems are, firstly, that we cannot necessarily access
our evaluation languages
\mathcal{E}_{\psi_{L},e}
and, secondly, that we
have to build a formula that may not depend on
k
.
As in Section
5.3.4
we define the product

e_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}=\psi_{L}(\ext_{x_{\ell},y_{\ell}}\circ\ext_{a_{\ell},b_{\ell}}\circ\cdots\circ\ext_{x_{\ell^{\prime}},y_{\ell^{\prime}}}\circ\ext_{a_{\ell^{\prime}},b_{\ell^{\prime}}})\quad\text{and}\quad e_{\ell}=e_{1,\ell}

for all
\ell,\ell^{\prime}\in[1,k]
.
We say an interval
I=[s,t]\subseteq[1,k]
is

repetitive
if
s<t
and
e_{s}=e_{t}
.

By Claim
5.14
there exist indices

1=t_{0}\leq s_{1}<t_{1}<s_{2}<t_{2}<\cdots<s_{q}<t_{q}\leq s_{q+1}=k
such that

[s_{1},t_{1}],\ldots,[s_{q},t_{q}]
are all repetitive and for

D_{0}=[t_{0},s_{1}],D_{1}=[t_{1},s_{2}],\dots,D_{q}=[t_{q},s_{q+1}]
we have

q+\sum_{p=0}^{q}\left|D_{p}\right|\leq 3\left|O_{L}\right|
.
Let
i=i_{1}<\cdots<i_{k}
and
j_{k}<\cdots<j_{1}=j
be the positions that
correspond to the positions of the letters
a_{1},\dots,a_{k}\in\Sigma_{\text{call}}

and
b_{k},\dots,b_{1}\in\Sigma_{\text{ret}}
of the factorization of

\ext_{w_{i}\cdots w_{i^{\prime}},w_{j^{\prime}}\cdots w_{j}}
in
w
, respectively: more
precisely
i_{\ell}=i+\mathopen{}\left|x_{1}\cdots a_{\ell-1}x_{\ell}\right|\mathclose{}
and

j_{\ell}=\mathopen{}\left|x_{1}a_{1}\cdots x_{k}a_{k}w_{i^{\prime}+1}\cdots w_{j^{\prime}-1}b_{k}y_{k}\cdots b_{\ell+1}y_{\ell+1}\right|\mathclose{}+1

for all
\ell\in[1,k]
.
Since the non-empty interval
[s_{p},t_{p}]
is repetitive for all
p\in[1,q]
,
we have
e_{s_{p}}=e_{t_{p}}
and thus obtain

e_{s_{p}}=e_{t_{p}}=e_{s_{p}}\circ\psi(\ext_{x_{s_{p}+1}\cdots a_{t_{p}},b_{t_{p}}\cdots y_{s_{p}+1}})\,\,\text{.}

Given
p\in[1,q]
, if
e_{s_{p}}\in\mathcal{Z}
, we can use the predicate

\pi_{e_{s_{p}}}^{\mathsf{exact}}
to check the above equality; we set

\theta_{d,e_{s_{p}}}(x,x^{\prime},y^{\prime},y)=\pi_{e_{s_{p}}}^{\mathsf{exact}}(x,x^{\prime},y^{\prime},y)
.
If
e_{s_{p}}\notin\mathcal{Z}
and is
\varphi_{L}(L)
-reaching, then

\mathcal{U}_{e_{s_{p}}}
is length-synchronous, so for all
e^{\prime}\in O_{L}
such
that
e_{s_{p}}\circ e^{\prime}=e_{s_{p}}
, we have that
e^{\prime}
is

\varphi_{L}(L)
-reaching and

\mathcal{R}_{e^{\prime}}=\{(u,v)\in\mathsf{Con}(\Sigma)\mid\psi_{L}(\ext_{u,v})=e^{\prime}\}

is length-synchronous. So to check the above equality, we can use the
formula
\theta_{d,e_{s_{p}}}(x,x^{\prime},y^{\prime},y)
built by taking the disjunction
over all
e^{\prime}\in O_{L}
such that
e_{s_{p}}\circ e^{\prime}=e_{s_{p}}
of the formulas
defined inductively completely analogously as
\chi_{d,e^{\prime}}(x,x^{\prime},y^{\prime},x^{\prime})

in Section
5.3.5
(using the sentence
\nu_{\psi_{L},e^{\prime}}

defining
\mathcal{E}_{\psi_{L},e^{\prime}}
): we simply replace every occurrence of

\eta_{d-1,r}
by
\overline{\eta}_{d-1,r}
.

Next, for all
m>0
and all
\varphi_{L}(L)
-reaching
f\in O_{L}
we will
construct a formula
\alpha_{d,m,f}(x,x^{\prime},y^{\prime},y)
such that for all

w\in\Sigma^{\triangle}
and

i,i^{\prime},j^{\prime},j\in[1,\mathopen{}\left|w\right|\mathclose{}],i\leq i^{\prime}<j^{\prime}\leq j
we have that if

w_{i}\cdots w_{i^{\prime}}w_{j^{\prime}}\cdots w_{j}\in\Sigma^{\triangle}
and
i^{\prime}
is
matched to
j^{\prime}
in
w
, then given

\ext_{w_{i}\cdots w_{i^{\prime}},w_{j^{\prime}}\cdots w_{j}}=\ext_{x_{1},y_{1}}\circ\ext_{a_{1},b_{1}}\circ\cdots\circ\ext_{x_{k},y_{k}}\circ\ext_{a_{k},b_{k}}

the stair factorization of
\ext_{w_{i}\cdots w_{i^{\prime}},w_{j^{\prime}}\cdots w_{j}}

provided by Lemma
3.6
, we have

(w,M^{\triangle}\left(w\right))\models\alpha_{d,m,f}(i,i^{\prime},j^{\prime},j)
if, and only
if,
\nd(x_{\ell}),\nd(y_{\ell})<d
for all
\ell\in[1,k]
,

\Delta(w_{i}\cdots w_{i^{\prime}})=-\Delta(w_{j^{\prime}}\cdots w_{j})=m
and

\psi_{L}(\ext_{w_{i}\cdots w_{i^{\prime}},w_{j^{\prime}}\cdots w_{j}})=e
.
For

\bm{\sigma}=(\bm{\sigma}_{1},\dots,\bm{\sigma}_{m})\in{\Sigma_{\text{call}}}^{m}
,

\bm{\xi}=(\bm{\xi}_{1}\dots,\bm{\xi}_{m})\in{\Sigma_{\text{ret}}}^{m}
,

\bm{r}=(\bm{r}_{1},\dots,\bm{r}_{m})\in{R_{L}}^{m}
, and

\bm{r}^{\dagger}=(\bm{r}_{1}^{\dagger},\dots,\bm{r}_{m}^{\dagger})\in{R_{L}}^{m}

we define

\prod(\bm{\sigma},\bm{\xi},\bm{r},\bm{r}^{\dagger})=\bigcirc_{g=1}^{m}\mathrm{left}_{\bm{r}_{g}}\circ\mathrm{right}_{\bm{r}_{g}^{\dagger}}\circ\psi_{L}(\ext_{\bm{\sigma}_{g},\bm{\xi}_{g}}).

The formula
\alpha_{d,m,f}
can be expressed as follows:

\displaystyle\alpha_{d,m,f}(x,x^{\prime},y^{\prime},y)=

\displaystyle\bigvee_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{\sigma}\in{\Sigma_{\text{call}}}^{m},\bm{\xi}\in{\Sigma_{\text{ret}}}^{m}\\
\bm{r},\bm{r}^{\dagger}\in{R_{L}}^{m}:f=\prod(\bm{\sigma},\bm{\xi},\bm{r},\bm{r}^{\dagger})\end{subarray}}\exists x_{1},\dots,x_{m}\exists y_{1},\dots,y_{m}

\displaystyle\biggl(\begin{aligned} &x^{\prime}=x_{m}\wedge y^{\prime}=y_{m}\wedge x\leq x_{1}<\cdots<x_{m}<y_{m}<\cdots<y_{1}\leq y\wedge\\
&\bigwedge_{g=1}^{m}\bigl(\bm{\sigma}_{g}(x_{g})\wedge\bm{\xi}_{g}(y_{g})\wedge x_{g}\leftrightsquigarrow y_{g}\bigr)\wedge\\
&\forall z\bigl((x\leq z\leq x^{\prime}\wedge\bigwedge_{g=1}^{m}z\neq x_{g})\rightarrow\neg U(x,x^{\prime},z)\bigr)\wedge\\
&\Bigl(\bigl(x<x_{1}\wedge\overline{\eta}_{d-1,\bm{r}_{1}}(x,x_{1}-1)\bigr)\vee F_{\bm{r}_{1}}(x,x_{1})\Bigr)\wedge\\
&\Bigl(\bigl(y_{1}<y\wedge\overline{\eta}_{d-1,\bm{r}_{1}^{\dagger}}(y_{1}+1,y)\bigr)\vee F_{\bm{r}_{1}^{\dagger}}(y_{1},y)\Bigr)\wedge\\
&\bigwedge_{g=2}^{m}\overline{\eta}_{d-1,\bm{r}_{g}}(x_{g-1}+1,x_{g}-1)\wedge\bigwedge_{g=2}^{m}\overline{\eta}_{d-1,\bm{r}_{g}^{\dagger}}(y_{g}+1,y_{g-1}-1)\biggr)\end{aligned}

with

F_{s}(x,z)=\begin{cases}x=z&\text{if $s=1_{R_{L}}$}\\
\bot&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

for all
\varphi_{L}(L)
-reaching
s\in R_{L}
.

We are now ready to define the formula
\overline{\chi}_{d,e}
:

\displaystyle\overline{\chi}_{d,e}(x,x^{\prime},y^{\prime},x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigvee_{q\in[0,\left|O_{L}\right|]\atop{m_{0},\dots,m_{q-1}\geq 1\wedge m_{q}\geq 0:\atop q+m_{0}+\dots+m_{q}\leq 3\left|O_{L}\right|}}\bigvee_{e_{0},f_{0},e_{1},\dots,f_{q-1},e_{q},f_{q}\in O_{L}\atop{e_{0}=1_{O_{L}}\wedge\forall j\in[1,q]:e_{j}=e_{j-1}\circ f_{j-1}\atop e=e_{q}\circ f_{q}}}

\displaystyle\exists x_{1},\dots,x_{q+1}\exists x_{0}^{\prime},\dots,x_{q}^{\prime}\exists y_{1},\dots,y_{q+1}\exists y_{1}^{\prime},\dots,y_{0}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\left(\Biggl.\!\!\!\!\!\phantom{\bigwedge_{p=1}^{q}}x_{0}^{\prime}\leq x_{1}<x_{1}^{\prime}<x_{2}<\dots<x_{q}^{\prime}\leq x_{q+1}<y_{q+1}\leq y_{q}^{\prime}<y_{q}<\dots<y_{1}^{\prime}<y_{1}\leq y_{0}^{\prime}\right.\wedge

\displaystyle\quad x_{0}^{\prime}=x\wedge y_{0}^{\prime}=y\wedge x_{q+1}=x^{\prime}\wedge y_{q+1}=y^{\prime}\wedge\bigwedge_{p=1}^{q}(x_{p}\leftrightsquigarrow y_{p}\wedge x_{p}^{\prime}\leftrightsquigarrow y_{p}^{\prime})\wedge

\displaystyle\quad\forall z\Bigl(\bigl(x\leq z<x^{\prime}\wedge\bigwedge_{p=1}^{q}(z\neq x_{p}\wedge z\neq x_{p}^{\prime})\bigr)\rightarrow\neg U(x,x^{\prime},z)\Bigr)\wedge

\displaystyle\quad\bigwedge_{p=1}^{q}\theta_{d,e_{p}}(x_{p}+1,x_{p}^{\prime},y_{p}^{\prime},y_{p}-1)\wedge

\displaystyle\quad\alpha_{d,m_{0},f_{0}}(x_{0}^{\prime},x_{1},y_{1},y_{0}^{\prime})\wedge\bigwedge_{p=1}^{q-1}\alpha_{d,m_{p},f_{p}}(x_{p}^{\prime}+1,x_{p+1},y_{p+1},y_{p}^{\prime}-1)\wedge

\displaystyle\quad\left.\Bigl(\bigl(x_{q}^{\prime}<x_{q+1}\wedge\alpha_{d,m_{q},f_{q}}(x_{q}^{\prime}+1,x_{q+1},y_{q+1},y_{q}^{\prime}-1)\bigr)\vee F^{\prime}_{q,m_{q},f_{q}}(x_{q}^{\prime},x_{q+1})\Bigr)\right)

with

F^{\prime}_{q,m,f}(x,z)=\begin{cases}\top&\text{if $q=0$}\\
x=z&\text{if $q>0$, $m=0$ and $f=1_{O_{L}}$}\\
\bot&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

for all
q\in[0,\left|O_{L}\right|]
,
m\in[0,3\left|O_{L}\right|]
and
f\in O_{L}
.

The inductive definition of
\overline{\eta}_{d,r}^{\uparrow}
is completely
analogous to the definition of
\eta_{d,r}
in
Section
5.3.5
: we simply replace every occurrence of

\eta_{d-1,r}
by
\overline{\eta}_{d-1,r}
and every occurrence of

\chi_{d,e}
by
\overline{\chi}_{d,e}
.

The inductive definition of
\overline{\eta}_{d,r}
is completely analogous
to the definition of
\eta_{d,r}
in Section
5.3.5
: we
access the horizontal evaluation languages
\mathcal{E}_{\varphi_{L},r}
for
all
\varphi_{L}(L)
-reaching
r\in R_{L}
by making use of the sentence

\nu_{\varphi,r}
and the already defined
\overline{\eta}_{d,r}^{\uparrow}
.
∎

5.4.2
Computation of
k,l

The following proposition implies the computability of
k,l\in\mathbb{N}

such that
\mathcal{L}_{k,l}\leq_{\mathrm{cd}}L
already when VPL
L
is
weakly length-synchronous but not length-synchronous.

Proposition 5.24
.

If a VPL
L
is weakly length-synchronous but not length-synchronous, one can effectively compute
k,l\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}
with
k\neq l
such that

\mathcal{L}_{k,l}\leq_{\mathrm{cd}}L
.

Proof.

Let

L\subseteq\Sigma^{\triangle}
be a weakly length-synchronous VPL that is not
length-synchronous.
According to Point 2 (b) of Proposition
5.1

one can effectively
compute a quadruple
(k_{0},l_{0},k_{0}^{\prime},l_{0}^{\prime})\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}^{4}

for which there exist

\ext_{u,v},\ext_{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{O}(\Sigma^{\triangle})
such that

•

|u|=k_{0}
,
|v|=l_{0}
,
|u^{\prime}|=k_{0}^{\prime}
,
|v^{\prime}|=l_{0}^{\prime}
,

•

\psi_{L}(\ext_{u,v})=\psi_{L}(\ext_{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}})
is a

\varphi_{L}(L)
-reaching idempotent,

•

\Delta(u),\Delta(u^{\prime})>0
,
and

•

\frac{k_{0}}{l_{0}}=\frac{\mathopen{}\left|u\right|\mathclose{}}{\mathopen{}\left|v\right|\mathclose{}}\neq\frac{\mathopen{}\left|u^{\prime}\right|\mathclose{}}{\mathopen{}\left|v^{\prime}\right|\mathclose{}}=\frac{k_{0}^{\prime}}{l_{0}^{\prime}}
.

We can explicitly compute such
\ext_{u,v}
and
\ext_{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}}
by just
doing an exhaustive search.
This enables us to assume without loss of generality while maintaining
effective computability that
\Delta(u)=\Delta(u^{\prime})
: indeed,
in case
\Delta(u)\not=\Delta(u^{\prime})
, we can consider

\ext_{u_{1},v_{1}}=\ext_{u^{\Delta(u^{\prime})},v^{\Delta(u^{\prime})}}

and
\ext_{u_{2},v_{2}}=\ext_{(u^{\prime})^{\Delta(u)},(v^{\prime})^{\Delta(u)}}

satisfying the desired properties.

Let us now define Green’s relations on
O_{L}

(see
[
25
, Chapter 3, Section 1]
).
Let us consider two elements
x,y
of
O_{L}
.

•

We write
x\leq_{\mathfrak{J}}y
whenever there are elements
e,f
of
O_{L}

such that
x=e\circ y\circ f
. We write
x\mathrel{\mathfrak{J}}y
if

x\leq_{\mathfrak{J}}y
and
y\leq_{\mathfrak{J}}x
. We write

x<_{\mathfrak{J}}y
if
x\leq_{\mathfrak{J}}y
and
x\not\mathrel{\mathfrak{J}}y
.

•

We write
x\leq_{\mathfrak{R}}y
whenever there is an element
e
of
O_{L}

such that
x=y\circ e
. We write
x\mathrel{\mathfrak{R}}y
if

x\leq_{\mathfrak{R}}y
and
y\leq_{\mathfrak{R}}x
.

•

We write
x\leq_{\mathfrak{L}}y
whenever there is an element
e
of
O_{L}

such that
x=e\circ y
. We write
x\mathrel{\mathfrak{L}}y
if

x\leq_{\mathfrak{L}}y
and
y\leq_{\mathfrak{L}}x
.

•

We write
x\mathrel{\mathfrak{H}}y
if
x\mathrel{\mathfrak{R}}y
and
x\mathrel{\mathfrak{L}}y
.

Observe that because
\Delta(u)=\Delta(u^{\prime})
, we have that

uv^{\prime}\in\Sigma^{\triangle}
and
u^{\prime}v\in\Sigma^{\triangle}
, so that we can
consider the elements

\ext_{uuu,vv^{\prime}v}=\ext_{u,v}\circ\ext_{u,v^{\prime}}\circ\ext_{u,v}

and

\ext_{uu^{\prime}u,vvv}=\ext_{u,v}\circ\ext_{u^{\prime},v}\circ\ext_{u,v}
in

\mathcal{O}(\Sigma^{\triangle})
.
These elements satisfy

\psi_{L}(\ext_{uuu,vv^{\prime}v})\leq_{\mathfrak{J}}\psi_{L}(\ext_{u,v})
and

\psi_{L}(\ext_{uu^{\prime}u,vvv})\leq_{\mathfrak{J}}\psi_{L}(\ext_{u,v})
. We claim that we
actually have
\psi_{L}(\ext_{uuu,vv^{\prime}v})<_{\mathfrak{J}}\psi_{L}(\ext_{u,v})
and

\psi_{L}(\ext_{uu^{\prime}u,vvv})<_{\mathfrak{J}}\psi_{L}(\ext_{u,v})
.
Indeed, assume we would have

\psi_{L}(\ext_{uu^{\prime}u,vvv})\mathrel{\mathfrak{J}}\psi_{L}(\ext_{u,v})
.
Set
x=\psi_{L}(\ext_{u,v})
and
y=\psi_{L}(\ext_{u^{\prime},v})
.
By a classical property of Green’s relations
(see
[
25
, Chapter 3, Proposition 1.4]
), since it would hold
that
x\circ y\circ x\leq_{\mathfrak{R}}x
and
x\circ y\circ x\mathrel{\mathfrak{J}}x
, we
would have
x\circ y\circ x\mathrel{\mathfrak{R}}x
and dually, since it would hold
that
x\circ y\circ x\leq_{\mathfrak{L}}x
and
x\circ y\circ x\mathrel{\mathfrak{J}}x
, we
would have
x\circ y\circ x\mathrel{\mathfrak{L}}x
.
Therefore, we would have
x\circ y\circ x\mathrel{\mathfrak{H}}x
. By another classical
result on Green’s relations
[
25
, Chapter 3, Corollary 1.7]
,
as
x
is an idempotent, its
\mathrel{\mathfrak{H}}
-class is a group, hence for

\omega\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}
the idempotent power of
O_{L}
, we would have

(x\circ y\circ x)^{\omega}=x^{\omega}=x
(as the only idempotent element
in a group is the identity). This would finally entail that

\psi_{L}(\ext_{(uu^{\prime}u)^{\omega},(vvv)^{\omega}})=\psi_{L}(\ext_{(uuu)^{\omega},(vvv)^{\omega}})

is a
\varphi_{L}(L)
-reaching idempotent and

\Delta((uu^{\prime}u)^{\omega})=\Delta((uuu)^{\omega})>0
but

\mathopen{}\left|(uu^{\prime}u)^{\omega}\right|\mathclose{}\neq\mathopen{}\left|(uuu)^{\omega}\right|\mathclose{}
, a contradiction to
the fact that
(\varphi_{L},\psi_{L})
is
\varphi_{L}(L)
-weakly-length-synchronous.
Symmetrically, we can prove that if we had

\psi_{L}(\ext_{uuu,vv^{\prime}v})\mathrel{\mathfrak{J}}\psi_{L}(\ext_{u,v})
, this would
contradict the fact that
(\varphi_{L},\psi_{L})
is

\varphi_{L}(L)
-weakly-length-synchronous.

We distinguish three cases. In each of these we prove that there exist

k,l\in\mathbb{N}_{>0},k\neq l
such that

\mathcal{L}_{k,l}\leq_{\mathrm{cd}}L_{\psi_{L}(\ext_{u,v})}
, so
that since
L_{\psi_{L}(\ext_{u,v})}\leq_{\mathrm{cd}}L
(by
Lemma
5.3
) and by transitivity of
\leq_{\mathrm{cd}}
we
have
\mathcal{L}_{k,l}\leq_{\mathrm{cd}}L
.

Case
\mathopen{}\left|v\right|\mathclose{}=\mathopen{}\left|v^{\prime}\right|\mathclose{}
.

In that case, we necessarily have
\mathopen{}\left|u\right|\mathclose{}\neq\mathopen{}\left|u^{\prime}\right|\mathclose{}
.
Then, we can exploit the fact that matching
u^{3}
with
vv^{\prime}v
or
uu^{\prime}u

with
v^{3}
makes us fall down to a smaller
\mathrel{\mathfrak{J}}
-class to reduce

\mathcal{L}_{3\mathopen{}\left|u\right|\mathclose{},2\mathopen{}\left|u\right|\mathclose{}+\mathopen{}\left|u^{\prime}\right|\mathclose{}}
to
L_{\psi_{L}(\ext_{u,v})}
.
The constant-depth reduction works as follows on input
w\in\Sigma^{*}
:

1.

check if
w=xy
with

x\in(ac^{3\mathopen{}\left|u\right|\mathclose{}-1}+ac^{2\mathopen{}\left|u\right|\mathclose{}+\mathopen{}\left|u^{\prime}\right|\mathclose{}-1})^{*}

and
y\in(b_{1}+b_{2})^{*}
,
reject if it’s not the case;

2.

build
x^{\prime}
by sending
ac^{3\mathopen{}\left|u\right|\mathclose{}-1}
to
u^{3}
,

ac^{2\mathopen{}\left|u\right|\mathclose{}+\mathopen{}\left|u^{\prime}\right|\mathclose{}-1}
to
uu^{\prime}u
and

y^{\prime}
by sending
b_{1}
to
v^{3}
and
b_{2}
to
vv^{\prime}v
;

3.

accept whenever
x^{\prime}\#y^{\prime}\in L_{\psi_{L}(\ext_{u,v})}
.

This forms a valid reduction. Indeed, take a word
w=xy
with

x\in(ac^{3\mathopen{}\left|u\right|\mathclose{}-1}+ac^{2\mathopen{}\left|u\right|\mathclose{}+\mathopen{}\left|u^{\prime}\right|\mathclose{}-1})^{n}
for

n\in\mathbb{N}
and
y\in(b_{1}+b_{2})^{m}
for
m\in\mathbb{N}
and consider
x^{\prime}\#y^{\prime}

produced by the reduction with
x^{\prime}\in(u^{3}+uu^{\prime}u)^{n}
and

y^{\prime}\in(v^{3}+vv^{\prime}v)^{m}
. If

w\in\mathcal{L}_{3\mathopen{}\left|u\right|\mathclose{},2\mathopen{}\left|u\right|\mathclose{}+\mathopen{}\left|u^{\prime}\right|\mathclose{}}
, then it easily
follows that
x^{\prime}\#y^{\prime}\in L_{\psi_{L}(\ext_{u,v})}
. Otherwise, if

w\notin\mathcal{L}_{3\mathopen{}\left|u\right|\mathclose{},2\mathopen{}\left|u\right|\mathclose{}+\mathopen{}\left|u^{\prime}\right|\mathclose{}}
, then either

n\neq m
and thus
x^{\prime}y^{\prime}
is not well-matched because

\Delta(x^{\prime})=n\cdot 3\cdot\Delta(u)
and

\Delta(y^{\prime})=m\cdot 3\cdot\Delta(v)
, or
n=m
and thus
x^{\prime}y^{\prime}
is
well-matched, so

\ext_{x^{\prime},y^{\prime}}=\ext_{z_{1}^{\prime},t_{1}^{\prime}}\circ\cdots\circ\ext_{z_{n}^{\prime},t_{n}^{\prime}}

with
z_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,z_{n}^{\prime}\in\{u^{3},uu^{\prime}u\}
and

t_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,t_{n}^{\prime}\in\{v^{3},vv^{\prime}v\}
such that there exists

i\in[1,n]
satisfying

\ext_{z_{i}^{\prime},t_{i}^{\prime}}\in\{\ext_{u^{3},vv^{\prime}v},\ext_{uu^{\prime}u,v^{3}}\}
,
thereby implying

\psi_{L}(\ext_{x^{\prime},y^{\prime}})\leq_{\mathfrak{J}}\psi_{L}(\ext_{z_{i}^{\prime},t_{i}^{\prime}})<_{\mathfrak{J}}\psi_{L}(\ext_{u,v})\,\,\text{.}

Our algorithm therefore outputs the pair
(k,l)=(3k_{0},2k_{0}+k_{0}^{\prime})
.

Case
\mathopen{}\left|u\right|\mathclose{}=\mathopen{}\left|u^{\prime}\right|\mathclose{}
.

This case is symmetric to the previous case.
Our algorithm outputs the pair
(k,l)=(2l_{0}+l_{0}^{\prime},3l_{0})
.

Case
\mathopen{}\left|u\right|\mathclose{}\neq\mathopen{}\left|u^{\prime}\right|\mathclose{}
and

\mathopen{}\left|v\right|\mathclose{}\neq\mathopen{}\left|v^{\prime}\right|\mathclose{}
.

Then, we can again exploit the fact that matching
u^{3}
with
vv^{\prime}v
or

uu^{\prime}u
with
v^{3}
makes us fall down to a smaller
\mathrel{\mathfrak{J}}
-class to
reduce
\mathcal{L}_{A\cdot B^{\prime},A^{\prime}\cdot B}
where
A=3\mathopen{}\left|u\right|\mathclose{}=3k_{0}
,

A^{\prime}=2\mathopen{}\left|u\right|\mathclose{}+\mathopen{}\left|u^{\prime}\right|\mathclose{}=2k_{0}+k_{0}^{\prime}
,
B=3\mathopen{}\left|v\right|\mathclose{}=3l_{0}
and

B^{\prime}=2\mathopen{}\left|v\right|\mathclose{}+\mathopen{}\left|v^{\prime}\right|\mathclose{}=2l_{0}+l_{0}^{\prime}
to
L_{\psi_{L}(\ext_{u,v})}
.
Indeed, we have
A\cdot B^{\prime}\neq A^{\prime}\cdot B
because otherwise we would have

\displaystyle 3\mathopen{}\left|u\right|\mathclose{}\cdot(2\mathopen{}\left|v\right|\mathclose{}+\mathopen{}\left|v^{\prime}\right|\mathclose{})

\displaystyle=(2\mathopen{}\left|u\right|\mathclose{}+\mathopen{}\left|u^{\prime}\right|\mathclose{})\cdot 3\mathopen{}\left|v\right|\mathclose{}

\displaystyle 6\mathopen{}\left|u\right|\mathclose{}\mathopen{}\left|v\right|\mathclose{}+3\mathopen{}\left|u\right|\mathclose{}\mathopen{}\left|v^{\prime}\right|\mathclose{}

\displaystyle=6\mathopen{}\left|u\right|\mathclose{}\mathopen{}\left|v\right|\mathclose{}+3\mathopen{}\left|u^{\prime}\right|\mathclose{}\mathopen{}\left|v\right|\mathclose{}

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\left|u\right|\mathclose{}\mathopen{}\left|v^{\prime}\right|\mathclose{}

\displaystyle=\mathopen{}\left|u^{\prime}\right|\mathclose{}\mathopen{}\left|v\right|\mathclose{}\,\,\text{.}

The constant-depth reduction works as follows on input
w\in\Sigma^{*}
:

1.

check if
w=xy
with

x\in(ac^{A\cdot B^{\prime}-1}+ac^{A^{\prime}\cdot B-1})^{*}
and

y\in(b_{1}+b_{2})^{*}
, reject if it is not the case;

2.

build
x^{\prime}
by sending
ac^{A\cdot B^{\prime}-1}
to
(u^{3})^{B^{\prime}}
,

ac^{A^{\prime}\cdot B-1}
to
(uu^{\prime}u)^{B}
and
y^{\prime}
by sending
b_{1}
to

(v^{3})^{B^{\prime}}
and
b_{2}
to
(vv^{\prime}v)^{B}
;

3.

accept whenever
x^{\prime}\#y^{\prime}\in L_{\psi_{L}(\ext_{u,v})}
.

This forms a valid reduction. Indeed, take a word
w=xy
with

x=z_{1}\cdots z_{n}
where
n\in\mathbb{N}
and

z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}\in\{ac^{A\cdot B^{\prime}-1},ac^{A^{\prime}\cdot B-1}\}

and
y=t_{1}\cdots t_{m}
where
m\in\mathbb{N}
and

t_{1},\ldots,t_{m}\in\{b_{1},b_{2}\}
. Consider
x^{\prime}\#y^{\prime}
produced by the
reduction with
x^{\prime}=z_{1}^{\prime}\cdots z_{n}^{\prime}
where

z_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,z_{n}^{\prime}\in\{(u^{3})^{B^{\prime}},(uu^{\prime}u)^{B}\}
and

y^{\prime}=t_{1}^{\prime}\cdots t_{m}^{\prime}
where

t_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,t_{m}^{\prime}\in\{(v^{3})^{B^{\prime}},(vv^{\prime}v)^{B}\}
.
If
w\in\mathcal{L}_{A\cdot B^{\prime},A^{\prime}\cdot B}
,
then it easily follows that
x^{\prime}\#y^{\prime}\in L_{\psi_{L}(\ext_{u,v})}
. Otherwise,
if
w\notin\mathcal{L}_{A\cdot B^{\prime},A^{\prime}\cdot B}
, three situations can occur.

•

There exists
i\in[1,\min\{n,m\}]
such that

z_{1}\cdots z_{i-1}t_{i-1}\cdots t_{1}\in\mathcal{L}_{A\cdot B^{\prime},A^{\prime}\cdot B}

but it holds that

(z_{i},t_{i})\in\{(ac^{A\cdot B^{\prime}-1},b_{2}),(ac^{A^{\prime}\cdot B-1},b_{1})\}
.
Assume first
(z_{i},t_{i})=(ac^{A^{\prime}\cdot B-1},b_{1})
.
In this case, let

\tilde{x}^{\prime}=(uu^{\prime}u)^{B-1}z_{i+1}^{\prime}\cdots z_{n}^{\prime}
and

\tilde{y}^{\prime}=t_{n}^{\prime}\cdots t_{i+1}^{\prime}(v^{3})^{B^{\prime}-1}
.
If
\Delta(\tilde{x}^{\prime}\tilde{y}^{\prime})\neq 0
, then

\Delta(x^{\prime}y^{\prime})=\Delta(z_{1}^{\prime}\cdots z_{i-1}^{\prime}(uu^{\prime}u)v^{3}t_{i-1}^{\prime}\cdots t_{1}^{\prime})+\Delta(\tilde{x}^{\prime}\tilde{y}^{\prime})=\Delta(\tilde{x}^{\prime}\tilde{y}^{\prime})\neq 0\,\,\text{,}

thus
x^{\prime}y^{\prime}
is not well-matched.
Otherwise, if
\Delta(\tilde{x}^{\prime}\tilde{y}^{\prime})=0
, we can show that

\tilde{x}^{\prime}\tilde{y}^{\prime}
is well-matched. Indeed, since

uv\in\Sigma^{\triangle}
, for all
j\in[1,\mathopen{}\left|u\right|\mathclose{}]
, we have

\Delta(u_{1}\cdots u_{j})\geq 0
and for all
j\in[1,\mathopen{}\left|v\right|\mathclose{}]
,
we have

\Delta(v_{j}\cdots v_{\mathopen{}\left|v\right|\mathclose{}})=-\Delta(uv_{1}\cdots v_{j-1})\leq 0
.
Similarly, since
u^{\prime}v^{\prime}\in\Sigma^{\triangle}
, for all

j\in[1,\mathopen{}\left|u^{\prime}\right|\mathclose{}]
, we have
\Delta(u^{\prime}_{1}\cdots u^{\prime}_{j})\geq 0

and for all
j\in[1,\mathopen{}\left|v^{\prime}\right|\mathclose{}]
, we have

\Delta(v^{\prime}_{j}\cdots v^{\prime}_{\mathopen{}\left|v^{\prime}\right|\mathclose{}})=-\Delta(u^{\prime}v^{\prime}_{1}\cdots v^{\prime}_{j-1})\leq 0
.
This implies that for all
j\in[1,\mathopen{}\left|\tilde{x}^{\prime}\right|\mathclose{}]
, we have

\Delta(\tilde{x}^{\prime}_{1}\cdots\tilde{x}^{\prime}_{j})\geq 0

and for all
j\in[1,\mathopen{}\left|\tilde{y}^{\prime}\right|\mathclose{}]
, we have

\Delta(\tilde{x}^{\prime}\tilde{y}^{\prime}_{1}\cdots\tilde{y}^{\prime}_{j-1})=-\Delta(\tilde{y}^{\prime}_{j}\cdots\tilde{y}^{\prime}_{\mathopen{}\left|\tilde{y}^{\prime}\right|\mathclose{}})\geq 0
.
Therefore,
\tilde{x}^{\prime}\tilde{y}^{\prime}\in\Sigma^{\triangle}
.
Hence
x^{\prime}y^{\prime}
is well-matched and

\ext_{x^{\prime},y^{\prime}}=\ext_{z_{1}^{\prime}\cdots z_{i-1}^{\prime},t_{i-1}^{\prime}\cdots t_{1}^{\prime}}\circ\ext_{z_{i}^{\prime},t_{i}^{\prime}}\circ\ext_{\tilde{x}^{\prime},\tilde{y}^{\prime}}\,\,\text{,}

so that

\psi_{L}(\ext_{x^{\prime},y^{\prime}})\leq_{\mathfrak{J}}\psi_{L}(\ext_{z_{i}^{\prime},t_{i}^{\prime}})<_{\mathfrak{J}}\psi_{L}(\ext_{u,v})\,\,\text{.}

If we assume that
(z_{i},t_{i})=(ac^{A\cdot B^{\prime}-1},b_{2})
, then we
prove in the same way that either
x^{\prime}y^{\prime}
is not well-matched or it
is well-matched and

\psi_{L}(\ext_{x^{\prime},y^{\prime}})<_{\mathfrak{J}}\psi_{L}(\ext_{u,v})
.

•

It holds that
n<m
and

z_{1}\cdots z_{n}t_{n}\cdots t_{1}\in\mathcal{L}_{A\cdot B^{\prime},A^{\prime}\cdot B}
.
This entails that

\Delta(x^{\prime}t_{n}^{\prime}\cdots t_{1}^{\prime})=\Delta(z_{1}^{\prime}\cdots z_{n}^{\prime}t_{n}^{\prime}\cdots t_{1}^{\prime})=0
,
so that

\Delta(x^{\prime}y^{\prime})=\Delta(x^{\prime}t_{n}^{\prime}\cdots t_{1}^{\prime})+\Delta(t_{m}^{\prime}\cdots t_{n+1}^{\prime})=\Delta(t_{m}^{\prime}\cdots t_{n+1}^{\prime})<0

because
m>n
and
\Delta(v)<0
as well as
\Delta(v^{\prime})<0
.
Therefore,
x^{\prime}y^{\prime}
is not well-matched.

•

It holds that
n>m
and

z_{1}\cdots z_{m}t_{m}\cdots t_{1}\in\mathcal{L}_{A\cdot B^{\prime},A^{\prime}\cdot B}
.
Symmetrically to the previous case, we can also show that then,

x^{\prime}y^{\prime}
is not well-matched.

Hence, our algorithm outputs
the pair

(k,l)=(A\cdot B^{\prime},A^{\prime}\cdot B)=(3k_{0}(2l_{0}+l_{0}^{\prime}),(2k_{0}+k_{0}^{\prime})3l_{0})

in this last case.
∎

5.5
Proof of Corollary
2.11

Let
A=(Q,\Sigma,q_{0},F,\delta_{0},\dots,\delta_{m})
be a
m
-VCA
and let
L=L(A)
.
One easily computes from
A^{\prime}
a DVPA such that
L(A^{\prime})=L
.
Details of this standard translation are omitted.
It will be sufficient to prove that
L
is weakly length-synchronous
if, and only if,
L
is length-synchronous: indeed,
one can simply perform the case distinction of Section
5.1

and observe that, under the assumption that weak length-synchronicity
and length synchronicity coincide, the algorithm for
Theorem
2.9

will either output that
L
is in
\mathsf{AC}^{0}
or some

m\geq 2
such that
\mathrm{MOD}_{m}\leq_{\mathrm{cd}}L
.

It thus suffices to prove that if
L
is not length-synchronous, then

L
is not weakly length-synchronous.
Let
(R_{L},O_{L})
be the syntactic
\Ext
-algebra of
L
along with
with its syntactic morphism

(\varphi_{L},\psi_{L})\colon(\Sigma^{\triangle},\mathcal{O}(\Sigma^{\triangle}))\rightarrow(R_{L},O_{L})
.

Let
\mathcal{M}=[0,m]\times(Q^{Q})^{[0,m]}\times(Q^{Q})^{[0,m]}
.
The behavior of the
m
-VCA can be described
as follows.
To each
\ext_{u,v}\in\mathcal{O}(\Sigma^{\triangle})
we assign
the triple
\zeta(\ext_{u,v})=(j,(f_{i})_{i\in[0,m]},(g_{i})_{i\in[0,m]})\in\mathcal{M}
, where

•

j=\min(\Delta(u),m)\in[0,m]
,

•

f_{i}(q)=q^{\prime}
where
q^{\prime}\in Q
is the unique state
such that
q(i)\xrightarrow{u}_{A}q^{\prime}(i+j)
for all
q\in Q
and
all
i\in[0,m]
, and

•

g_{i}(q)=q^{\prime}
, where
q^{\prime}\in Q
is the unique state
such that
q^{\prime}(i+j)\xrightarrow{v}_{A}q^{\prime}(i)
for all
q\in Q
and all
i\in[0,m]
.

Over
\mathcal{M}
we define the product

(j,(f_{i})_{i\in[0,m]},(g_{i})_{i\in[0,m]})\odot_{\mathcal{M}}(j^{\prime},(f_{i}^{\prime})_{i\in[0,m]},(g_{i}^{\prime})_{i\in[0,m]})

as

(\min(j+j^{\prime},m),(f_{\min(i+j,m)}^{\prime}\circ f_{i})_{i\in[0,m]},(g_{\min(i+j,m)}\circ g_{\min(i+j+j^{\prime},m)}^{\prime})_{i\in[0,m]}).

We claim
\odot_{\mathcal{M}}
is associative.
For this, let us fix

•

m=(j,(f_{i})_{i\in[0,m]},(g_{i})_{i\in[0,m]})\in\mathcal{M}
,

•

m^{\prime}=(j^{\prime},(f_{i}^{\prime})_{i\in[0,m]},(g_{i}^{\prime})_{i\in[0,m]})\in\mathcal{M}
, and

•

m^{\prime\prime}=(j^{\prime\prime},(f_{i}^{\prime\prime})_{i\in[0,m]},(g_{i}^{\prime\prime})_{i\in[0,m]})\in\mathcal{M}
.

Let
\widetilde{m}=m\odot_{\mathcal{M}}m^{\prime}=(\widetilde{j},(\widetilde{f_{i}})_{i\in[0,m]},(\widetilde{g_{i}})_{i\in[0,m]})

and

\overline{m}=m^{\prime}\odot_{\mathcal{M}}m^{\prime\prime}=(\overline{j},(\overline{f_{i}})_{i\in[0,m]},(\overline{g_{i}})_{i\in[0,m]})
.
We need to prove
\widetilde{m}\odot_{\mathcal{M}}m^{\prime\prime}=m\odot_{\mathcal{M}}\overline{m}
.

Since
j,j^{\prime},j^{\prime\prime},m\geq 0
we have

\min(\widetilde{j}+j^{\prime\prime},m)=\min(\min(j+j^{\prime},m)+j^{\prime\prime},m)=\min(j+j^{\prime}+j^{\prime\prime},m)=\min(j+\min(j^{\prime}+j^{\prime\prime},m),m))=\min(j+\overline{j},m)

associativity holds on the first component.
For each
i\in[0,m]
the
i^{\text{th}}
component of the second
component of
\widetilde{m}\odot_{\mathcal{M}}m^{\prime\prime}
is

\displaystyle f_{\min(i+\widetilde{j},m)}^{\prime\prime}\circ\widetilde{f}_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f_{\min(i+\widetilde{j},m)}^{\prime\prime}\circ\left(f_{\min(i+j,m)}^{\prime}\circ f_{i}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f_{\min(i+\min(j+j^{\prime},m),m)}^{\prime\prime}\circ\left(f_{\min(i+j,m)}^{\prime}\circ f_{i}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f_{\min(i+j+j^{\prime},m)}^{\prime\prime}\circ\left(f_{\min(i+j,m)}^{\prime}\circ f_{i}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(f_{\min(i+j^{\prime}+j^{\prime\prime},m)}^{\prime\prime}\circ f_{\min(i+j,m)}^{\prime}\right)\circ f_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(f_{\min(\min(i+j,m)+j^{\prime},m)}^{\prime\prime}\circ f_{\min(i+j,m)}^{\prime}\right)\circ f_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\overline{f}_{\min(i+j,m)}\circ f_{i}

the latter of which is the
i^{\text{th}}
component of the second
component of
m\odot_{\mathcal{M}}\overline{m}
, as required.
For each
i\in[0,m]
the
i^{\text{th}}
component of the third
component of
m\odot_{\mathcal{M}}\overline{m}
is

\displaystyle g_{\min(i+j,m)}\circ\overline{g}_{\min(i+j+\overline{j},m))}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g_{\min(i+j,m)}\circ\overline{g}_{\min(i+j+j^{\prime}+j^{\prime\prime},m)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g_{\min(i+j,m)}\circ\overline{g}_{\min(\min(i+j,m)+j^{\prime}+j^{\prime\prime},m)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g_{\min(i+j,m)}\circ\left(g_{\min(\min(i+j,m)+j^{\prime})}^{\prime}\circ g_{\min(\min(i+j,m),j^{\prime}+j^{\prime\prime},m)}^{\prime\prime}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(g_{\min(i+j,m)}\circ g_{\min(\min(i+j,m)+j^{\prime})}^{\prime}\right)\circ g_{\min(\min(i+j,m),j^{\prime}+j^{\prime\prime},m)}^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(g_{\min(i+j,m)}\circ g_{\min(i+j+j^{\prime},m)}^{\prime}\right)\circ g_{\min(i+\min(j+j^{\prime},m)+j^{\prime\prime},m)}^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\widetilde{g}_{i}\circ g_{\min(i+\widetilde{j}+j^{\prime\prime},m)}^{\prime\prime}

the latter of which is the
i^{\text{th}}
component of the third
component of
\widetilde{m}\odot_{\mathcal{M}}m^{\prime\prime}
, as required.
Clearly
(0,(\mathrm{id}_{Q})^{[0,m]},(\mathrm{id}_{Q})^{[0,m]})
is the identity of
\mathcal{M}

with respect to
\odot_{\mathcal{M}}
, hence
(\mathcal{M},\odot_{\mathcal{M}})
is a monoid.
The following points can easily be verified.

1.

The function
\zeta\colon\mathcal{O}(\Sigma^{\triangle})\rightarrow\mathcal{M}

is a monoid morphism.

2.

For all
\ext_{u,v},\ext_{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{O}(\Sigma^{\triangle})

with
\Delta(u)=\Delta(u^{\prime})

we have

\zeta(\ext_{u,v})=\zeta(\ext_{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}})\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\zeta(\ext_{u,v})=\zeta(\ext_{u^{\prime},v})=\zeta(\ext_{u,v^{\prime}})=\zeta(\ext_{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}}).

3.

For all
\ext_{u,v},\ext_{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{O}(\Sigma^{\triangle})

we have

\zeta(\ext_{u,v})=\zeta(\ext_{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}})\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\psi_{L}(\ext_{u,v})=\psi_{L}(\ext_{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}}).

Now assume that
L
is not length-synchronous. We will prove
that
L
is not length-synchronous.
By assumption there exist a
\varphi_{L}(L)
-reaching idempotent
e\in O_{L}

and
\ext_{u,v},\ext_{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{O}(\Sigma^{\triangle})

such that
\Delta(u),\Delta(u^{\prime})>0
,
\frac{|u|}{|v|}\not=\frac{|u^{\prime}|}{|v^{\prime}|}
,
and
\psi_{L}(\ext_{u,v})=\psi_{L}(\ext_{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}})=e
.
Without loss of generality we may assume
\Delta(u)=\Delta(u^{\prime})
.
Let
\omega
denote the idempotent power of
\mathcal{M}
.
Consider the elements

\ext_{x,y}=(\ext_{u,v}^{2\omega}\circ\ext_{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}}^{\omega})^{\omega}\text{\lx@nobreakspace and }\ext_{x^{\prime},y^{\prime}}=(\ext_{u,v}^{\omega}\circ\ext_{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}}^{2\omega})^{\omega}.

By definition we have

\zeta(\ext_{x,y})=\zeta(\ext_{x^{\prime},y^{\prime}})
,
and since
\Delta(x)=\Delta(x^{\prime})
we obtain

\zeta(\ext_{x,y})=\zeta(\ext_{x^{\prime},y})=\zeta(\ext_{x,y^{\prime}})=\zeta(\ext_{x^{\prime},y^{\prime}})

by Point 2.
Hence,

\displaystyle\psi_{L}(\ext_{x,y})=\psi_{L}(\ext_{x^{\prime},y})=\psi_{L}(\ext_{x,y^{\prime}})=\psi_{L}(\ext_{x^{\prime},y^{\prime}})=e

by Point 3.

We finally make a case distinction on whether
|u|=|u^{\prime}|
or not.

First, assume
|u|\not=|u^{\prime}|
.
Then
|x|\not=|x^{\prime}|
by construction.
Since

\psi_{L}(\ext_{x,y})=\psi_{L}(\ext_{x^{\prime},y})=e
, which
is an idempotent of
O_{L}
, we obtain that
L
is not weakly length-synchronous.

Now assume
|u|=|u^{\prime}|
.
Since
\frac{|u|}{|v|}\not=\frac{|u^{\prime}|}{|v^{\prime}|}
by assumption,
we conclude that
|v|\not=|v^{\prime}|
.
By construction, the latter implies
|y|\not=|y^{\prime}|
.
Since
\psi_{L}(\ext_{x,y})=\psi_{L}(\ext_{x,y^{\prime}})=e
,
again we obtain that
L
is not weakly length-synchronous.

6
Proof of Proposition
5.1

We will prove the different statements appearing in
Proposition
5.1
in the following subsections.

Computability of the syntactic
\Ext
-algebra.

This paragraph will be devoted to proving Point 1 of
Proposition
5.1
, rephrased in the following proposition.

Proposition 6.1
.

Given a DVPA
A
with
L=L(A)
, one can compute the syntactic
\Ext
-algebra

(R_{L},O_{L})
of
L
, its syntactic morphism
(\varphi_{L},\psi_{L})
and

\varphi_{L}(L)
.

We require a bit of notation.
For each visibly pushdown alphabet
\Sigma
and each finite
\Ext
-algebra

(R,O)
it follows from Proposition
3.7
that
each morphism

(\varphi,\psi)\colon(\Sigma^{\triangle},\mathcal{O}(\Sigma^{\triangle}))\rightarrow(R,O)

has a unique finite presentation: it is given by the tuples

(\varphi(c))_{c\in\Sigma_{\text{int}}}\quad\text{and}\quad(\psi(\ext_{a,b}))_{(a,b)\in\Sigma_{\text{call}}\times\Sigma_{\text{ret}}}\quad.

The syntactic
\Ext
-algebra
(R_{L},O_{L})
of a VPL
L
over a visibly pushdown
alphabet
\Sigma
can be represented by any
\Ext
-algebra
(R,O)
such that

R
has
[1,\left|R_{L}\right|]
as base set and such that there exists a bijective
morphism
(\alpha,\beta)\colon(R,O)\to(R_{L},O_{L})
. Indeed, in that case we
have

1.

xy=z\Leftrightarrow\alpha(x)\alpha(y)=\alpha(z)
for all

x,y,z\in R
;

2.

x^{\prime}y^{\prime}=z^{\prime}\Leftrightarrow\alpha^{-1}(x^{\prime})\alpha^{-1}(y^{\prime})=\alpha^{-1}(z^{\prime})

for all
x^{\prime},y^{\prime},z^{\prime}\in R_{L}
;

3.

f(x)=y\Leftrightarrow\beta(f)(\alpha(x))=\alpha(y)
for all

f\in O
and all
x,y\in R
; and

4.

f^{\prime}(x^{\prime})=y^{\prime}\Leftrightarrow\beta^{-1}(f^{\prime})(\alpha^{-1}(x^{\prime}))=\alpha^{-1}(y^{\prime})

for all
f^{\prime}\in O_{L}
and all
x^{\prime},y^{\prime}\in R_{L}
.

For the following claim we avoid the tedious standard algebraic constructions on
\Ext
-algebras
to show decidability of the equivalence problem, since
the latter decidability has already been established in
[
2
]
.

Claim 6.2
.

There is an algorithm that decides, given two morphisms into finite

\Ext
-algebras

(\varphi_{1},\psi_{1})\colon(\Sigma^{\triangle},\mathcal{O}(\Sigma^{\triangle}))\rightarrow(R_{1},O_{1})

and

(\varphi_{2},\psi_{2})\colon(\Sigma^{\triangle},\mathcal{O}(\Sigma^{\triangle}))\rightarrow(R_{1},O_{1})

for
\Sigma
a visibly pushdown alphabet and subsets
F_{1}\subseteq R_{1}
and

F_{2}\subseteq R_{2}
, whether
\varphi_{1}^{-1}(F_{1})=\varphi_{2}^{-1}(F_{2})
.

Proof of the Claim.

The proof of Theorem
3.18
shows that one can effectively
compute DVPAs
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
such that
L(A_{1})=\varphi_{1}^{-1}(F_{1})

and
L(A_{2})=\varphi_{2}^{-1}(F_{2})
.
By
[
2
]
one can effectively decide if
L(A_{1})=L(A_{2})

by deciding
L(A_{1})\subseteq L(A_{2})
and
L(A_{2})\subseteq L(A_{1})
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
6.1
.

By Theorem
3.18
we first compute from our DVPA
A
on the
visibly pushdown alphabet
\Sigma
an
\Ext
-algebra
(R_{A},O_{A})
, a morphism

(\varphi_{A},\psi_{A})\colon(\Sigma^{\triangle},\mathcal{O}(\Sigma^{\triangle}))\to(R_{A},O_{A})
,
and a subset
F_{A}\subseteq R_{A}
such that
L(A)=\varphi_{A}^{-1}(F_{A})
.
For an
\Ext
-algebra
(R,O)
define
\#(R,O)=(\left|R\right|,\left|O\right|)
.
Let
\prec\subseteq(\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N})^{2}
be the lexicographic order on
\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}
,
i.e.
(i,j)\prec(k,l)
if, and only if either
i<k
, or
i=k
and
j<l
.

Observe that since
(R_{A},O_{A})
recognizes
L
, we have that the syntactic

\Ext
-algebra
(R_{L},O_{L})
of
L
divides
(R_{A},O_{A})
by
Proposition
3.17
, so that

\#(R_{L},O_{L})\leq\#(R_{A},O_{A})
.
In fact, we have that any
\Ext
-algebra
(R,O)
having
[1,i]
for

i\in[1,\left|R_{L}\right|]
as base set, satisfying
\#(R,O)\leq\#(R_{L},O_{L})

and recognizing
L
via a morphism

(\varphi,\psi)\colon(\Sigma^{\triangle},\mathcal{O}(\Sigma^{\triangle}))\to(R,O)

is a presentation of
(R_{L},O_{L})
with
(\varphi,\psi)
and
F

presentations of, respectively,
(\varphi_{L},\psi_{L})
and
\varphi_{L}(L)
.
Indeed, since such an
\Ext
-algebra recognizes
L
, by
Proposition
3.17
it is divided by
(R_{L},O_{L})
:
this implies that
\#(R_{L},O_{L})\leq\#(R,O)
, but as also

\#(R,O)\leq\#(R_{L},O_{L})
, we have
\#(R,O)=\#(R_{L},O_{L})
.
The morphism
(\varphi,\psi)
must be surjective, otherwise, by
Lemma
3.15
,

\bigl(\varphi(\Sigma^{\triangle}),{\psi(\mathcal{O}(\Sigma^{\triangle}))|}_{\varphi(\Sigma^{\triangle})}\bigr)

would be a sub-
\Ext
-algebra of
(R,O)
recognizing
L
such that

\#\bigl(\varphi(\Sigma^{\triangle}),{\psi(\mathcal{O}(\Sigma^{\triangle}))|}_{\varphi(\Sigma^{\triangle})}\bigr)<\#(R,O)=\#(R_{L},O_{L})

while
(R_{L},O_{L})
divides

\bigl(\varphi(\Sigma^{\triangle}),{\psi(\mathcal{O}(\Sigma^{\triangle}))|}_{\varphi(\Sigma^{\triangle})}\bigr)
,
which is contradictory.
Therefore, by Lemma
3.16
, there is
a surjective morphism
(\alpha,\beta)\colon(R,O)\to(R_{L},O_{L})
, that
must be bijective, such that
\varphi_{L}=\alpha\circ\varphi
, so that

(R,O)
is a presentation of
(R_{L},O_{L})
with
(\varphi,\psi)
and
F

presentations of, respectively,
(\varphi_{L},\psi_{L})
and
\varphi_{L}(L)
.

Under the assumption that such an
\Ext
-algebra exists, we compute

(R_{L},O_{L})
,
(\varphi_{L},\psi_{L})
and
\varphi_{L}(L)
by enumerating all
the finitely many triples made of a finite
\Ext
-algebra
(R,O)
, a
morphism

(\varphi,\psi)\colon(\Sigma^{\triangle},\mathcal{O}(\Sigma^{\triangle}))\to(R,O)

and a subset
F\subseteq R
such that
R
has
[1,i]
for

i\in[1,\left|R_{A}\right|]
as base set and
\#(R,O)\leq\#(R_{A},O_{A})
.
For each of these we test whether
\varphi^{-1}(F)=\varphi_{A}^{-1}(F_{A})
,
which is possible by the above claim and take
(R,O)
,
(\varphi,\psi)

and
F
from a triple validating this test with
\#(R,O)
minimal with
respect to
\prec
.

It remains to prove that an
\Ext
-algebra
(R,O)
having
[1,i]
for

i\in[1,\left|R_{L}\right|]
as base set, satisfying
\#(R,O)\leq\#(R_{L},O_{L})

and recognizing
L
exists.
Take any bijection
\alpha\colon R_{L}\to[1,\left|R_{L}\right|]
. We define
R
to
be the monoid with base set
[1,\left|R_{L}\right|]
and operation defined by

x\cdot y=\alpha\bigl(\alpha^{-1}(x)\alpha^{-1}(y)\bigr)
for all

x,y\in[1,\left|R_{L}\right|]
. This is a monoid because

•

x\cdot\alpha(1_{R})=\alpha\bigl(\alpha^{-1}(x)\alpha^{-1}(\alpha(1_{R}))\bigr)=\alpha(\alpha^{-1}(x))=\alpha\bigl(\alpha^{-1}(\alpha(1_{R}))\alpha^{-1}(x)\bigr)=\alpha(1_{R})\cdot x

for all
x\in R
; and

•

for all
x,y,z\in R
, we have

\displaystyle x\cdot(y\cdot z)

\displaystyle=\alpha\biggl(\alpha^{-1}(x)\alpha^{-1}\Bigl(\alpha\bigl(\alpha^{-1}(y)\alpha^{-1}(z)\bigr)\Bigr)\biggr)

\displaystyle=\alpha\bigl(\alpha^{-1}(x)\alpha^{-1}(y)\alpha^{-1}(z)\bigr)

\displaystyle=\alpha\biggl(\alpha^{-1}\Bigl(\alpha\bigl(\alpha^{-1}(x)\alpha^{-1}(y)\bigr)\Bigr)\alpha^{-1}(z)\biggr)

\displaystyle=(x\cdot y)\cdot z\,\,\text{.}

Define the function
\beta\colon O_{L}\to R^{R}
by

\beta(f^{\prime})(x)=\alpha\bigl(f^{\prime}(\alpha^{-1}(x))\bigr)
for all
f^{\prime}\in O_{L}

and
x\in R
. Set
O
to be the monoid with base set
\beta(O_{L})
and with
composition as operation. This is a monoid because

•

\beta(1_{O})(x)=\alpha\bigl(1_{O}(\alpha^{-1}(x))\bigr)=x=\mathrm{id}_{R}(x)

for all
x\in R
; and

•

for all
f^{\prime},g^{\prime}\in O_{L}
,

\beta(f^{\prime})\circ\beta(g^{\prime})(x)=\alpha\Biggl(f^{\prime}\biggl(\alpha^{-1}\Bigl(\alpha\bigl(g^{\prime}(\alpha^{-1}(x))\bigr)\Bigr)\biggr)\Biggr)=\alpha\bigl(f^{\prime}\circ g^{\prime}(\alpha^{-1}(x))\bigr)=\beta(f^{\prime}\circ g^{\prime})(x)

for all
x\in R
, so that
\beta(f^{\prime})\circ\beta(g^{\prime})\in O
.

Then
(R,O)
is an
\Ext
-algebra, because for all
r^{\prime}\in R_{L}
, we have

\beta(\mathrm{left}_{r^{\prime}})(x)=\alpha\bigl(\mathrm{left}_{r^{\prime}}(\alpha^{-1}(x))\bigr)=\alpha\bigl(\alpha^{-1}(\alpha(r^{\prime}))\alpha^{-1}(x)\bigr)=\alpha(r^{\prime})\cdot x=\mathrm{left}_{\alpha(r^{\prime})}(x)

for all
x\in R
and
\beta(\mathrm{right}_{r^{\prime}})(x)=\mathrm{right}_{\alpha(r^{\prime})}(x)

for all
x\in R
, so that
\mathrm{left}_{r},\mathrm{right}_{r}\in O
for all

r\in R
by surjectivity of
\alpha
.

We now define

(\varphi,\psi)\colon(\Sigma^{\triangle},\mathcal{O}(\Sigma^{\triangle}))\to(R,O)

as the unique morphism satisfying
\varphi(c)=\alpha(\varphi_{L}(c))
for all

c\in\Sigma_{\text{int}}
and
\psi(\ext_{a,b})=\beta(\psi_{L}(\ext_{a,b}))
for
all
a\in\Sigma_{\text{call}}
,
b\in\Sigma_{\text{ret}}
given by
Proposition
3.7
.
It is easy to show that then,
\varphi(w)=\alpha(\varphi_{L}(w))
for all

w\in\Sigma^{\triangle}
by structural induction on
w
.
Hence, by injectivity of
\alpha
, we have

\displaystyle\varphi^{-1}\bigl(\alpha(\varphi_{L}(L))\bigr)

\displaystyle=\{w\in\Sigma^{\triangle}\mid\alpha(\varphi_{L}(w))\in\alpha(\varphi_{L}(L))\}

\displaystyle=\{w\in\Sigma^{\triangle}\mid\varphi_{L}(w)\in\varphi_{L}(L)\}=\varphi_{L}^{-1}(\varphi_{L}(L))=L\,\,\text{,}

thus
(R,O)
recognizes
L
.
∎

Decidability of quasi-aperiodicity.

This paragraph is devoted to proving Point 2 (a)
of Proposition
5.1
,
rephrased in the following proposition.

Proposition 6.3
.

Given a morphism

(\varphi,\psi)\colon(\Sigma^{\triangle},\mathcal{O}(\Sigma^{\triangle}))\to(R,O)

for
\Sigma
a visibly pushdown alphabet and
(R,O)
a finite

\Ext
-algebra, it is decidable if
(\varphi,\psi)
is quasi-aperiodic.
If
(\varphi,\psi)
is not quasi-aperiodic, one can effectively
compute
k,l\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\psi(\mathcal{O}(\Sigma^{\triangle})^{k,l})
is
not aperiodic.

For the rest of this paragraph, let us fix a morphism

(\varphi,\psi)\colon(\Sigma^{\triangle},\mathcal{O}(\Sigma^{\triangle}))\to(R,O)
,
where
\Sigma
is a visibly pushdown alphabet and
(R,O)
is some finite

\Ext
-algebra that is the input to our problem.
We first have the following lemma.

Lemma 6.4
.

For all
e\in O
one can effectively compute a finite
\Ext
-algebra
recognizing

L_{e}=\{u\#v\mid uv\in\Sigma^{\triangle}:\psi(\ext_{u,v})=e\}
,
where
\#
is a fresh internal letter that does not appear in
\Sigma
,
along with an associated morphism and subset.

Proof.

Let
\Sigma^{\prime}
be the alphabet that emerges from
\Sigma
by
additionally declaring
\#
as an internal letter.
We will construct an
\Ext
-algebra
(R^{\prime},O^{\prime})
and a morphism

(\varphi^{\prime},\psi^{\prime})\colon(\Sigma^{\prime\triangle},\mathcal{O}(\Sigma^{\prime})^{\triangle})\to(R^{\prime},O^{\prime})

such that for some element
r^{\prime}\in R^{\prime}
we have
L_{e}=\varphi^{\prime-1}(r^{\prime})
.

We define
R^{\prime}=R\cup O\cup\{\bot\}
, for some fresh zero
\bot
,
where multiplication between two elements in
R^{\prime}
is
defined as follows:

•

multiplication between two elements in
R
is inherited from the
monoid
R
;

•

r\cdot f=\mathrm{left}_{r}\circ f
and
f\cdot r=\mathrm{right}_{r}\circ f

for all
r\in R
and all
f\in O
;

•

\bot
acts as a zero, i.e.
\bot\cdot r^{\prime}=r^{\prime}\cdot\bot=\bot
for all

r^{\prime}\in R^{\prime}
;

•

f\cdot g=\bot
for all
f,g\in O
.

Clearly the identity of
R
is the identity of
R^{\prime}
.
Associativity is immediate except for products of the form

r_{1}\cdot f\cdot r_{2},r_{1}\cdot r_{2}\cdot f
, and
f\cdot r_{1}\cdot r_{2}
,
where
f\in O
and
r_{1},r_{2}\in R
.
In the first case we have

\displaystyle(r_{1}\cdot f)\cdot r_{2}

\displaystyle=(\mathrm{left}_{r_{1}}\circ f)\cdot r_{2}=\mathrm{right}_{r_{2}}\circ(\mathrm{left}_{r_{1}}\circ f)=(\mathrm{right}_{r_{2}}\circ\mathrm{left}_{r_{1}})\circ f

\displaystyle=(\mathrm{left}_{r_{1}}\circ\mathrm{right}_{r_{2}})\circ f=r_{1}\cdot(\mathrm{right}_{r_{2}}\circ f)=r_{1}\cdot(f\cdot r_{2})\,\,\text{.}

In the second case we have

(r_{1}\cdot r_{2})\cdot f=\mathrm{left}_{r_{1}r_{2}}\circ f=(\mathrm{left}_{r_{1}}\circ\mathrm{left}_{r_{2}})\circ f=\mathrm{left}_{r_{1}}\circ(\mathrm{left}_{r_{2}}\circ f)=r_{1}\cdot(r_{2}\cdot f)

and in the third case we have

f\cdot(r_{1}\cdot r_{2})=\mathrm{right}_{r_{1}r_{2}}\circ f=(\mathrm{right}_{r_{2}}\circ\mathrm{right}_{r_{1}})\circ f=\mathrm{right}_{r_{2}}\circ(\mathrm{right}_{r_{1}}\circ f)=(f\cdot r_{1})\cdot r_{2}\,\,\text{.}

We define
O^{\prime}=(R^{\prime})^{R^{\prime}}
which is clearly a monoid for composition and thus
directly get that
(R^{\prime},O^{\prime})
is an
\Ext
-algebra.
Applying Proposition
3.7
, we define the morphism

(\varphi^{\prime},\psi^{\prime})\colon(\Sigma^{\prime\triangle},\mathcal{O}(\Sigma^{\prime})^{\triangle})\to(R^{\prime},O^{\prime})

as the unique one satisfying
\varphi^{\prime}(c)=\varphi(c)
for all

c\in\Sigma_{\text{int}}
,
\varphi^{\prime}(\#)=\mathrm{id}_{O}

and where for all
a\in\Sigma_{\text{call}}
,
b\in\Sigma_{\text{ret}}
, we have

\psi^{\prime}(\ext_{a,b})(x)=\begin{cases}\psi(\ext_{a,b})(x)&\text{if $x\in R$}\\
\psi(\ext_{a,b})\circ x&\text{if $x\in O$}\\
\bot&\text{otherwise (i.e.\ if $x=\bot$)}\end{cases}

for all
x\in R^{\prime}
.
It suffices to prove the following claim, which directly implies the desired
equality

\varphi^{\prime-1}(e)=\{u\#v\mid uv\in\Sigma^{\triangle}\text{ s.t.\ }\psi(\ext_{u,v})=e\}
.
For all
w\in\Sigma^{\prime\triangle}
we have

\varphi^{\prime}(w)=\begin{cases}\varphi(w)&\text{ if $w\in\Sigma^{\triangle}$}\\
\psi(\ext_{u,v})&\text{ if $w=u\#v$ for some $uv\in\Sigma^{\triangle}$}\\
\bot&\text{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

We prove it by structural induction on
w
. The cases when
w=\varepsilon

or
w=c\in\Sigma_{\text{int}}
follow immediately from the definition of

\varphi^{\prime}
.
In case
w=\#=\varepsilon\#\varepsilon
, we have

\varphi^{\prime}(w)=\mathrm{id}_{O}=\psi(\ext_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon})
.

For the inductive step first assume

w=aw^{\prime}b
for some
w^{\prime}\in\Sigma^{\prime\triangle}
.
If
w^{\prime}
is neither in
\Sigma^{\triangle}
nor of the form

u\#v
with
uv\in\Sigma^{\triangle}
, then
\varphi^{\prime}(w^{\prime})=\bot
by
induction hypothesis and thus

\varphi^{\prime}(w)=\psi^{\prime}(\ext_{a,b})(\varphi^{\prime}(w^{\prime}))=\psi^{\prime}(\ext_{a,b})(\bot)=\bot

as required.
If
w^{\prime}\in\Sigma^{\triangle}
, then
\varphi^{\prime}(w^{\prime})=\varphi(w^{\prime})\in R
by
induction hypothesis, and hence

\varphi^{\prime}(w)=\psi^{\prime}(\ext_{a,b})(\varphi^{\prime}(w^{\prime}))=\psi^{\prime}(\ext_{a,b})(\varphi(w^{\prime}))=\psi(\ext_{a,b})(\varphi(w^{\prime}))=\varphi(w)

as required.
If
w^{\prime}=u\#v
with
uv\in\Sigma^{\triangle}
, i.e.
w=au\#vb
, then

\varphi^{\prime}(w^{\prime})=\psi(\ext_{u,v})\in O
by induction hypothesis.
Hence, we have
\varphi^{\prime}(w)=\psi^{\prime}(\ext_{a,b})(\varphi^{\prime}(w^{\prime}))=\psi^{\prime}(\ext_{a,b})(\psi(\ext_{u,v}))=\psi(\ext_{a,b})\circ\psi(\ext_{u,v})=\psi(\ext_{au,vb})
.

Finally assume
w=xy
for some
x,y\in\Sigma^{\prime\triangle}\setminus\{\varepsilon\}
.
The case when
x
or
y
is neither in
\Sigma^{\triangle}
nor of the form

u\#v
with
uv\in\Sigma^{\triangle}
is easily handled by applying the
induction hypothesis and observing that
\bot
is a zero in
R^{\prime}
.
Two other immediate cases are when both
x
and
y
are in

\Sigma^{\triangle}
and when both
x
and
y
are of the form
u\#v
with

uv\in\Sigma^{\triangle}
.
Consider the case when
x\in\Sigma^{\triangle}\setminus\{\varepsilon\}

and
y=u\#v
with
uv\in\Sigma^{\triangle}
, hence
w=xu\#v
.
The induction hypothesis yields
\varphi^{\prime}(x)=\varphi(x)\in R
and

\varphi^{\prime}(y)=\psi(\ext_{u,v})\in O
.
We obtain

\varphi^{\prime}(xy)=\varphi^{\prime}(x)\cdot\varphi^{\prime}(y)=\varphi(x)\cdot\psi(\ext_{u,v})=\mathrm{left}_{\varphi(x)}\circ\psi(\ext_{u,v})=\psi(\ext_{xu,v})

as required.
Finally, let us treat the case when
x=u\#v
with

uv\in\Sigma^{\triangle}
and

y\in\Sigma^{\triangle}\setminus\{\varepsilon\}
, i.e.
w=u\#vy
.
The induction hypothesis yields
\varphi^{\prime}(x)=\psi(\ext_{u,v})\in O
and

\varphi^{\prime}(y)=\varphi(y)\in R
.
We obtain

\varphi^{\prime}(xy)=\varphi^{\prime}(x)\cdot\varphi^{\prime}(y)=\psi(\ext_{u,v})\cdot\varphi(y)=\mathrm{right}_{\varphi(y)}\circ\psi(\ext_{u,v})=\psi(\ext_{u,vy})

as required.
∎

The next goal will be to prove that the set of pairs of word
lengths
(\mathopen{}\left|u\right|\mathclose{},\mathopen{}\left|v\right|\mathclose{})
of words
u\#v\in L_{e}
is effectively
semilinear for each
e\in O
.

A
(realtime) pushdown automaton (PDA for short)
is a tuple

A=(Q,\Sigma,\Gamma,\Omega,q_{0},F,\bot)
,
where
Q
is a finite set of
states
,

\Sigma
is a finite
input alphabet
,

\Gamma
is a finite
stack alphabet
,

q_{0}\in Q
is an
initial state
,

F\subseteq Q
is the set of
final states
,

\bot\in\Gamma\setminus\Sigma
is the
bottom-of-stack symbol
,
and
\Omega\subseteq Q\times\Sigma\times\Gamma\times Q\times\Gamma^{*}
is a
finite
transition relation
such that for all
(p,a,X,q,\alpha)\in\Omega

we have
\alpha\in\Gamma^{*}\bot
if
X=\bot
and

\alpha\in(\Gamma\setminus\{\bot\})^{*}
otherwise.
The relation
\Omega
is naturally extended to
the relation
\Omega^{*}\subseteq Q\times\Sigma^{*}\times\Gamma^{*}\bot\times Q\times\Gamma^{*}\bot
,
namely as the smallest relation containing the set

\{(p,\varepsilon,\alpha,p,\alpha)\mid p\in Q,\alpha\in\Gamma^{*}\bot\}

and such that moreover, if
(p,a,X,q,\alpha)\in\Omega
and

(q,w,\alpha\beta,r,\gamma)\in\Omega^{*}
, then

(p,aw,X\beta,r,\gamma)\in\Omega^{*}
.
The
language
of
A
is

L(A)=\{w\in\Sigma^{*}\mid\exists\alpha\in\Gamma^{*}\bot,\exists q\in F:(q_{0},w,\bot,q,\alpha)\in\Omega^{*}\}
.
Hence it is clear that one can compute
a PDA
A^{\prime}
such that
L(A^{\prime})=L(A)
.

Lemma 6.5
.

Let
A
be a DVPA that accepts a language over a visibly pushdown alphabet

\Sigma^{\prime}
such that

L(A)\subseteq\{u\#v\mid uv\in\Sigma^{\prime\triangle}\setminus\Sigma^{\prime*}\#\Sigma^{\prime*}\}

and
\#\in\Sigma^{\prime}_{\text{int}}
.
Then the set

P(L(A))=\{(k,l)\in\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}\mid\exists u\in(\Sigma^{\prime}\setminus\{\#\})^{k},v\in(\Sigma^{\prime}\setminus\{\#\})^{l}:u\#v\in L(A)\}

is effectively semilinear.

Proof.

We first compute a PDA
A^{\prime}
accepting the same language as
A
, i.e.
L(A^{\prime})=L(A)
.
Let us assume without loss of generality that
0,1\not\in\Sigma^{\prime}
.
We claim that from
A^{\prime}=(Q,\Sigma^{\prime},\Gamma,\Omega,q_{0},F,\bot)
one can
compute a PDA
A^{\prime\prime}
such that

L(A^{\prime\prime})=\{0^{|u|}\#1^{|v|}\mid u\#v\in L(A^{\prime})\}\,\,\text{.}

Indeed, the PDA
A^{\prime\prime}
can simply be computed as follows:
we set

A^{\prime\prime}=(Q\times\{0,1\},\{0,1,\#\},\Gamma,\Omega^{\prime},\langle q_{0},0\rangle,F\times\{1\},\bot)\,\,\text{,}

where
\Omega^{\prime}
is the union of

\bigl\{(\langle p,i\rangle,i,X,\langle q,i\rangle,\alpha)\mathrel{\big|}i\in\{0,1\},\exists c\in\Sigma^{\prime}\setminus\{\#\}:(p,c,X,q,\alpha)\in\Omega\bigr\}

and
\{(\langle p,0\rangle,\#,X,\langle q,1\rangle,\alpha)\mid(p,\#,X,q,\alpha)\in\Omega\}
.
Finally, we apply Parikh’s Theorem, cf.
[
14
, Section 3]
, which
implies that the set
\{(m,n)\in\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}\mid 0^{m}\#1^{n}\in L(A^{\prime\prime})\}=P(L(A))

is effectively semilinear.
∎

We are now ready to prove Proposition
6.3
.

Proof of Proposition
6.3
.

Let
e\in O
.
By Lemma
6.4
we first compute a finite
\Ext
-algebra
recoginizing
L_{e}
, along with an associated morphism and subset. From the
latter we can compute (by Theorem
3.18
) a DVPA
A_{e}
accepting

L_{e}
. We then use Lemma
6.5
to conclude that the set

P(L_{e})=\{(k,l)\in\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}\mid\exists u\in\Sigma^{k},v\in\Sigma^{l}:uv\in\Sigma^{\triangle},\psi(\ext_{u,v})=e\}

is effectively semilinear, and this holds for all
e\in O
.

We make use of the folklore fact that semilinear sets are effectively closed
under Boolean operations, cf.
[
9
]
for a recent study.
To decide whether
(\varphi,\psi)
is quasi-aperiodic, we go through all
possible subsets
U\subseteq O
: if it is a subsemigroup of
O
that is a
non-trivial group, we compute the set
\bigcap_{e\in U}P(L_{e})
and reject
if it is non-empty (which is easy to check given a semilinear presentation
of the set), otherwise we continue. If we were able to go through
all those subsets without rejecting, we accept.

Thus, if
(\varphi,\psi)
is not quasi-aperiodic we can
find a subset
U\subseteq O
that contains a non-trivial group and
output a pair
(k,l)\in\bigcap_{e\in U}P(L_{e})
;
it witnesses that
\psi(\mathcal{O}(\Sigma^{\triangle})^{k,l})

is not aperiodic.
∎

Decidability of length-synchronicity.

This paragraph is devoted to proving Point 2 (b)
of Proposition
5.1
,
rephrased in the following proposition.

Proposition 6.6
.

Given a morphism

(\varphi,\psi)\colon(\Sigma^{\triangle},\mathcal{O}(\Sigma^{\triangle}))\to(R,O)
,
for
\Sigma
a visibly pushdown alphabet and
(R,O)
a finite

\Ext
-algebra, and some
F\subseteq R
, it is decidable if
(\varphi,\psi)

is
F
-length-synchronous.
If
(\varphi,\psi)
is not length-synchronous, one
can effectively compute a tuple
(k,l,k^{\prime},l^{\prime})\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}^{4}

such that that there exist
uv,u^{\prime}v^{\prime}\in\Sigma^{\triangle}

and some
F
-reaching idempotent
e\in O

such that
\psi(\ext_{u,v})=\psi(\ext_{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}})=e
,

k=|u|
,
l=|v|
,
k^{\prime}=|u^{\prime}|
,
l^{\prime}=|v^{\prime}|
and

\frac{k}{l}\not=\frac{k^{\prime}}{l^{\prime}}
.

Before proving the proposition we need a technical lemma
characterizing when a two-dimensional semilinear set
contains only vectors with the same slope.
We say two vectors
\vec{x},\vec{y}\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
are
collinear
if

\vec{y}=\alpha\cdot\vec{x}
for some
\alpha\in\mathbb{Q}_{>0}

Lemma 6.7
.

Let
S=\bigcup_{i\in I}\left(\vec{x}_{i,0}+\sum_{j=1}^{t_{i}}\mathbb{N}\vec{x}_{i,j}\right)\subseteq\mathbb{N}_{>0}^{2}
be a non-empty semilinear set,
where

\vec{x}_{i,j}\not=(0,0)
for all
i\in I
and all
j\in[0,t_{i}]
.
Then,

\left|\left\{\frac{k}{l}\Big|(k,l)\in S\right\}\right|=1\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\forall i,i^{\prime}\in I\ \forall j\in[0,t_{i}]\ \forall j^{\prime}\in[0,t_{i^{\prime}}]:\vec{x}_{i,j}\text{ and }\vec{x}_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}\text{ are collinear}.

Proof.

First assume that

\vec{x}_{i,j}
and
\vec{x}_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}
are collinear for all

i,i^{\prime}\in I
,
j\in[0,t_{i}]
, and
j^{\prime}\in[0,t_{i^{\prime}}]
.
Let
(k,l),(k^{\prime},l^{\prime})\in S
.
That is,
(k,l)=\vec{x}_{i,0}+n_{1}\vec{x}_{i,1}+\dots+n_{t_{i}}\vec{x}_{i,t_{i}}

and
(k^{\prime},l^{\prime})=\vec{x}_{i^{\prime},0}+n_{1}^{\prime}\vec{x}_{i^{\prime},1}+\dots+n_{t_{i^{\prime}}}^{\prime}\vec{x}_{i^{\prime},t_{i^{\prime}}}

for some
i,i^{\prime}\in I
and some
n_{1},\dots,n_{t_{i}},n_{1}^{\prime},\dots,n_{t_{i^{\prime}}}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
.
But due to pairwise collinearity there exist

\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Q}_{>0}
such that
(k,l)=\alpha\vec{x}_{i,0}

and
(k^{\prime},l^{\prime})=\alpha^{\prime}\vec{x}_{i,0}
, thus implying

\frac{k}{l}=\frac{k^{\prime}}{l^{\prime}}
.

Conversely assume that there exist two vectors
(k,l)=\vec{x}_{i,j}

and
(k^{\prime},l^{\prime})=\vec{x}_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}
that are not collinear.
In case this is possible when
i\not=i^{\prime}
and
j=j^{\prime}=0
we are done,
since then
(k,l),(k^{\prime},l^{\prime})\in S
and thus
\frac{k}{l}\not=\frac{k^{\prime}}{l^{\prime}}
.
Otherwise
\vec{x}_{i,0}
and
\vec{x}_{i^{\prime},0}
are collinear
for all
i,i^{\prime}\in I
, so there must exist

i\in I
and
j\in[0,t_{i}]

such that
\vec{x}_{i,0}
and
\vec{x}_{i,j}
are not collinear.
Then
\vec{x}_{i,0}
and
\vec{x}_{i,0}+\vec{x}_{i,j}
are in
S

but also not collinear: indeed, if

\alpha\vec{x}_{i,0}=\vec{x}_{i,0}+\vec{x}_{i,j}
for some
\alpha\in\mathbb{Q}_{>0}
,
then
\vec{x}_{i,j}=(\alpha-1)\vec{x}_{i,0}
with
\alpha-1>0

due to
\vec{x}_{i,0},\vec{x}_{i,j}\in\mathbb{N}^{2}\setminus\{(0,0)\}
,
a contradiction.
Hence there exist
(k,l),(k^{\prime},l^{\prime})\in S
that are not collinear,
and therefore
\frac{k}{l}\not=\frac{k^{\prime}}{l^{\prime}}
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
6.6
.

Let us fix the
\Ext
-algebra morphism

(\varphi,\psi)\colon(\Sigma^{\triangle},\mathcal{O}(\Sigma^{\triangle}))\to(R,O)
,
where
(R,O)
is a finite
\Ext
-algebra and where
F\subseteq R
.

Recall that over the alphabet
\Sigma^{\prime}
,
obtained from
\Sigma
by declaring a fresh
letter
\#
as internal, the language

L_{e\uparrow}=\{u\#v\mid uv\in\Sigma^{\triangle}:\Delta(u)>0,\psi(\ext_{u,v})=e\}=L_{e}\cap\{u\#v\mid uv\in\Sigma^{\triangle}:\Delta(u)>0\}

is given for all
e\in O
.
The language
\{u\#v\mid uv\in\Sigma^{\triangle}\lx@nobreakspace:\Delta(u)>0\}
is a clearly
a VPL.
Thus, for all
e\in O
, we have that the set

P(L_{e\uparrow})=\left\{(k,l)\in\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}\mid\exists u\in\Sigma^{k},v\in\Sigma^{l}:uv\in\Sigma^{\triangle},\Delta(u)>0,\psi(\ext_{u,v})=e\right\}

is effectively semilinear: indeed, given
e\in O
, using Lemma
6.4

and Theorem
3.18
, we can as in the proof of Point 2 (a)
of Proposition
5.1
compute a DVPA
A_{e}
accepting

L_{e}
; we then compute a DVPA
A_{e\uparrow}
accepting

L_{e\uparrow}=L(A_{e})\cap L(A)
by using the effective construction given
in
[
2
]
and finally use Lemma
6.5
to conclude.

Observe that
(\varphi,\psi)
is
F
-length-synchronous
if, and only if, for each
F
-reaching idempotent
e\in O

for which
P(L_{e\uparrow})
is non-empty we have

|\{\frac{k}{l}\mid(k,l)\in P(L_{e\uparrow})\}|=1.

The latter condition is easily seen to be decidable
by the characterization provided in Lemma
6.7
.
Hence, for deciding if
(\varphi,\psi)
is length-synchronous
our algorithm verifies if for all

F
-reaching
e\in O
for which
P(L_{e\uparrow})
is non-empty
we have

|\{\frac{k}{l}\mid(k,l)\in P(L_{e\uparrow})\}|=1.

On the other hand, if this verification fails, i.e.
in case
(\varphi,\psi)
is not
F
-length-synchronous,
our algorithm outputs,
again using the characterization of Lemma
6.7
,
a quadruple

(k,l,k^{\prime},l^{\prime})\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}^{4}
such that
for some
F
-reaching idempotent
e\in O
we have

(k,l),(k^{\prime},l^{\prime})\in P(L_{e\uparrow})
and

\frac{k}{l}\not=\frac{k^{\prime}}{l^{\prime}}
.
∎

Decidability of weak length-synchronicity.

This paragraph is devoted to proving Point 2 (c)
of Proposition
5.1
,
rephrased in the following proposition.

Proposition 6.8
.

Given a morphism

(\varphi,\psi)\colon(\Sigma^{\triangle},\mathcal{O}(\Sigma^{\triangle}))\to(R,O)
,
for
\Sigma
a visibly pushdown alphabet and
(R,O)
a finite

\Ext
-algebra, and some
F\subseteq R
, it is decidable if
(\varphi,\psi)

is
F
-weakly-length-synchronous.

Let us fix the morphism

(\varphi,\psi)\colon(\Sigma^{\triangle},\mathcal{O}(\Sigma^{\triangle}))\to(R,O)
,
for
\Sigma
a visibly pushdown alphabet and
(R,O)
a finite
\Ext
-algebra,
and some
F\subseteq R
.

Define the new visibly pushdown alphabet
\overline{\Sigma}
by

\overline{\Sigma}_{\text{call}}=\{\overline{b}\mid b\in\Sigma_{\text{ret}}\}
,

\overline{\Sigma}_{\text{int}}=\{\overline{c}\mid c\in\Sigma_{\text{int}}\}

and
\overline{\Sigma}_{\text{ret}}=\{\overline{a}\mid a\in\Sigma_{\text{call}}\}
.
For all
w\in\Sigma^{*}
, we define

\overline{w}=\begin{cases}\varepsilon&\text{if $w=\varepsilon$}\\
\overline{w_{n}}\cdots\overline{w_{1}}&\text{if $w=w_{1}\cdots w_{n}$ for $n\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}$ and
$w_{1},\ldots w_{n}\in\Sigma$}\,\,\text{.}\end{cases}

We have the following lemma, that we prove later on.

Lemma 6.9
.

For all
e\in O
one can effectively compute a finite
\Ext
-algebra
recognizing the language of well-matched words

K_{e}=\{u\#\overline{u^{\prime}}\mid u,u^{\prime}\in\Sigma^{*},\exists v\in\Sigma^{*}:uv\in\Sigma^{\triangle},u^{\prime}v\in\Sigma^{\triangle},\psi(\ext_{u,v})=\psi(\ext_{u^{\prime},v})=e\}
,
where
\#
is a fresh internal letter that does not appear in

\Sigma\cup\overline{\Sigma}
, along with an associated morphism and
subset.

Over the alphabet
\Sigma^{\prime}
obtained from
\Sigma\cup\overline{\Sigma}
by
declaring the fresh letter
\#
as internal, we define

\displaystyle K_{e\uparrow}

\displaystyle=\biggl\{u\#\overline{u^{\prime}}\mathrel{\bigg|}\begin{aligned} &u,u^{\prime}\in\Sigma^{*},\exists v\in\Sigma^{*}:\\
&uv\in\Sigma^{\triangle},u^{\prime}v\in\Sigma^{\triangle},\Delta(u)>0,\psi(\ext_{u,v})=\psi(\ext_{u^{\prime},v})=e\end{aligned}\biggr\}

\displaystyle=K_{e}\cap\{u\#u^{\prime}\mid uu^{\prime}\in(\Sigma\cup\overline{\Sigma})^{\triangle}:\Delta(u)>0\}

for all
e\in O
.
As in the proof of Point (2) of the second statement of
Proposition
5.1
, we can prove that the language

\{u\#u^{\prime}\mid uu^{\prime}\in(\Sigma\cup\overline{\Sigma})^{\triangle}:\Delta(u)>0\}

is a VPL and thus conclude that for all
e\in O
, the set

P(K_{e\uparrow})=\biggl\{(k,l)\in\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}\mathrel{\bigg|}\begin{aligned} &\exists u\in\Sigma^{k},u^{\prime}\in\Sigma^{l},v\in\Sigma^{*}:\\
&uv\in\Sigma^{\triangle},u^{\prime}v\in\Sigma^{\triangle},\Delta(u)>0,\psi(\ext_{u,v})=\psi(\ext_{u^{\prime},v})=e\end{aligned}\biggr\}

is effectively semilinear.

It is clear that
(\varphi,\psi)
is
F
-weakly-length-synchronous if and only
if for each idempotent
e\in O
that is
F
-reaching, there does not exist any

(x_{1},x_{2})\in P(K_{e\uparrow})
such that
x_{1}\neq x_{2}
.
Therefore, to decide whether
(\varphi,\psi)
is
F
-weakly-length-synchronous,
we go through all
e\in O
: if
e
is an idempotent that is
F
-reaching, we
compute the set
P(K_{e\uparrow})
and reject if it contains a vector

(x_{1},x_{2})
such that
x_{1}\neq x_{2}
(which is easy to check given a semilinear
presentation of the set), otherwise we continue. Finally, if we were able to go
through all those elements without rejecting, we accept.

Proof of Lemma
6.9
.

Let
\Sigma^{\prime}
be the alphabet that emerges from

\Sigma\cup\overline{\Sigma}
by additionally declaring
\#
as an
internal letter.
We will construct an
\Ext
-algebra
(R^{\prime},O^{\prime})
and a morphism

(\varphi^{\prime},\psi^{\prime})
from
(\Sigma^{\prime\triangle},\mathcal{O}(\Sigma^{\prime})^{\triangle})
to

(R^{\prime},O^{\prime})
such that for some subset
F\subseteq R^{\prime}
we have

K_{e}=\varphi^{\prime-1}(F)
.

Let
\overline{R}=\{\overline{r}\mid r\in R\}
.
We define

R^{\prime}=R\cup\overline{R}\cup\mathfrak{P}(O^{2})\setminus\emptyset\cup\{\bot,1\}
,
for some fresh zero
\bot
and identity
1
, where multiplication between two elements in
R^{\prime}
is
defined as follows:

•

for all
r_{1},r_{2}\in R
,

\displaystyle r_{1}\cdot r_{2}

\displaystyle=r_{1}r_{2}

\displaystyle\overline{r_{1}}\cdot r_{2}

\displaystyle=\bot

\displaystyle\overline{r_{1}}\cdot\overline{r_{2}}

\displaystyle=\overline{r_{2}r_{1}}

\displaystyle r_{1}\cdot\overline{r_{2}}

\displaystyle=\bot\,\,\text{;}

•

for all
r\in R
and
E\in\mathfrak{P}(O^{2})\setminus\emptyset
,

\displaystyle r\cdot E

\displaystyle=\{(\mathrm{left}_{r}\circ e_{1},e_{2})\mid(e_{1},e_{2})\in E\}

\displaystyle E\cdot r

\displaystyle=\bot

\displaystyle E\cdot\overline{r}

\displaystyle=\{(e_{1},\mathrm{left}_{r}\circ e_{2})\mid(e_{1},e_{2})\in E\}

\displaystyle\overline{r}\cdot E

\displaystyle=\bot\,\,\text{;}

•

for all
E_{1},E_{2}\in\mathfrak{P}(O^{2})\setminus\emptyset
, we have

E_{1}\cdot E_{2}=\bot
;

•

\bot
acts as a zero, i.e.
\bot\cdot r^{\prime}=r^{\prime}\cdot\bot=\bot
for all

r^{\prime}\in R^{\prime}
;

•

1
acts as an identity, i.e.
1\cdot r^{\prime}=r^{\prime}\cdot 1=r^{\prime}
for
all
r^{\prime}\in R^{\prime}
.

Associativity is immediate except for products of the form

\overline{r_{1}}\cdot\overline{r_{2}}\cdot\overline{r_{3}}
,

r_{1}\cdot E\cdot\overline{r_{2}}
,
r_{1}\cdot r_{2}\cdot E
and

E\cdot\overline{r_{1}}\cdot\overline{r_{2}}
,
where
E\in\mathfrak{P}(O^{2})\setminus\emptyset
and
r_{1},r_{2},r_{3}\in R
.
In the first case we have

(\overline{r_{1}}\cdot\overline{r_{2}})\cdot\overline{r_{3}}=\overline{r_{2}r_{1}}\cdot\overline{r_{3}}=\overline{r_{3}r_{2}r_{1}}=\overline{r_{1}}\cdot\overline{r_{3}r_{2}}=\overline{r_{1}}\cdot(\overline{r_{2}}\cdot\overline{r_{3}})\,\,\text{.}

In the second case we have

\displaystyle(r_{1}\cdot E)\cdot\overline{r_{2}}

\displaystyle=\{(\mathrm{left}_{r_{1}}\circ e_{1},e_{2})\mid(e_{1},e_{2})\in E\}\cdot\overline{r_{2}}

\displaystyle=\{(\mathrm{left}_{r_{1}}\circ e_{1},\mathrm{left}_{r_{2}}\circ e_{2})\mid(e_{1},e_{2})\in E\}

\displaystyle=r_{1}\cdot\{(e_{1},\mathrm{left}_{r_{2}}\circ e_{2})\mid(e_{1},e_{2})\in E\}=r_{1}\cdot(E\cdot\overline{r_{2}})\,\,\text{.}

In the third case we have

\displaystyle(r_{1}\cdot r_{2})\cdot E

\displaystyle=\{(\mathrm{left}_{r_{1}r_{2}}\circ e_{1},e_{2})\mid(e_{1},e_{2})\in E\}

\displaystyle=\{(\mathrm{left}_{r_{1}}\circ\mathrm{left}_{r_{2}}\circ e_{1},e_{2})\mid(e_{1},e_{2})\in E\}

\displaystyle=r_{1}\cdot\{(\mathrm{left}_{r_{2}}\circ e_{1},e_{2})\mid(e_{1},e_{2})\in E\}=r_{1}\cdot(r_{2}\cdot E)

and in the fourth case we have

\displaystyle E\cdot(\overline{r_{1}}\cdot\overline{r_{2}})

\displaystyle=\{(e_{1},\mathrm{left}_{r_{2}r_{1}}\circ e_{2})\mid(e_{1},e_{2})\in E\}

\displaystyle=\{(e_{1},\mathrm{left}_{r_{2}}\circ\mathrm{left}_{r_{1}}\circ e_{2})\mid(e_{1},e_{2})\in E\}

\displaystyle=\{(e_{1},\mathrm{right}_{r_{1}}\circ e_{2})\mid(e_{1},e_{2})\in E\}\cdot\overline{r_{2}}=(E\cdot\overline{r_{1}})\cdot\overline{r_{2}}\,\,\text{.}

We define
O^{\prime}=(R^{\prime})^{R^{\prime}}
which is clearly a monoid for composition and thus
directly get that
(R^{\prime},O^{\prime})
is a finite
\Ext
-algebra.
Applying Proposition
3.7
, we define the morphism

(\varphi^{\prime},\psi^{\prime})\colon(\Sigma^{\prime\triangle},\mathcal{O}(\Sigma^{\prime})^{\triangle})\to(R^{\prime},O^{\prime})

as the unique one satisfying
\varphi^{\prime}(c)=\varphi(c)
and

\varphi^{\prime}(\overline{c})=\overline{\varphi(c)}
for all

c\in\Sigma_{\text{int}}
,

\varphi^{\prime}(\#)=\{(\psi(\ext_{\varepsilon,v}),\psi(\ext_{\varepsilon,v}))\mid v\in\Sigma^{\triangle}\}

and where for all
a,a^{\prime}\in\Sigma_{\text{call}}
,
b,b^{\prime}\in\Sigma_{\text{ret}}
, we
have

\displaystyle\psi^{\prime}(\ext_{a,b})(x)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\psi(\ext_{a,b})(1_{R})&\text{if $x=1$}\\
\psi(\ext_{a,b})(x)&\text{if $x\in R$}\\
\bot&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\psi^{\prime}(\ext_{\overline{b^{\prime}},\overline{a^{\prime}}})(x)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\overline{\psi(\ext_{a^{\prime},b^{\prime}})(1_{R})}&\text{if $x=1$}\\
\overline{\psi(\ext_{a^{\prime},b^{\prime}})(x^{\prime})}&\text{if $x=\overline{x^{\prime}}$ for $x^{\prime}\in R$}\\
\bot&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\psi^{\prime}(\ext_{a,\overline{a^{\prime}}})(x)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}b\in\Sigma_{\text{ret}}\\
z\in\Sigma^{\triangle}\\
(e_{1},e_{2})\in x\end{subarray}}\{(\psi(\ext_{a,bz})\circ e_{1},\psi(\ext_{a^{\prime},bz})\circ e_{2})\}&\text{if $x\in\mathfrak{P}(O^{2})\setminus\emptyset$}\\
\bot&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\psi^{\prime}(\ext_{\overline{b^{\prime}},b})(x)

\displaystyle=\bot

for all
x\in R^{\prime}
.
Note that
(\varphi^{\prime},\psi^{\prime})
is computable because

\displaystyle\{(\psi(\ext_{\varepsilon,v}),\psi(\ext_{\varepsilon,v}))\mid v\in\Sigma^{\triangle}\}

\displaystyle=\{(\mathrm{right}_{\varphi(v)},\mathrm{right}_{\varphi(v)})\mid v\in\Sigma^{\triangle}\}

\displaystyle=\{(\mathrm{right}_{r},\mathrm{right}_{r})\mid r\in R\}

and

\displaystyle\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}b\in\Sigma_{\text{ret}}\\
z\in\Sigma^{\triangle}\\
(e_{1},e_{2})\in x\end{subarray}}\{(\psi(\ext_{a,bz})\circ e_{1},\psi(\ext_{a^{\prime},bz})\circ e_{2})\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}b\in\Sigma_{\text{ret}}\\
z\in\Sigma^{\triangle}\\
(e_{1},e_{2})\in x\end{subarray}}\{(\mathrm{right}_{\varphi(z)}\circ\psi(\ext_{a,b})\circ e_{1},\mathrm{right}_{\varphi(z)}\circ\psi(\ext_{a^{\prime},b})\circ e_{2})\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}b\in\Sigma_{\text{ret}}\\
r\in R\\
(e_{1},e_{2})\in x\end{subarray}}\{(\mathrm{right}_{r}\circ\psi(\ext_{a,b})\circ e_{1},\mathrm{right}_{r}\circ\psi(\ext_{a^{\prime},b})\circ e_{2})\}

for all
x\in\mathfrak{P}(O^{2})\setminus\emptyset
and

a,a^{\prime}\in\Sigma_{\text{call}}
.

Now define the set of pairs

P=\{(u,u^{\prime})\in\Sigma^{*}\times\Sigma^{*}\mid\exists v\in\Sigma^{*}:uv\in\Sigma^{\triangle},u^{\prime}v\in\Sigma^{\triangle}\}
;
it is not difficult to check that for all
w\in\Sigma^{\prime*}
,

w\in\Sigma^{\prime\triangle}\cap\Sigma^{*}\#\overline{\Sigma}^{*}
if and only
if
w=u\#\overline{u^{\prime}}
for
(u,u^{\prime})\in P
.
It suffices to prove the following claim, which directly implies the desired
equality

\displaystyle\varphi^{\prime-1}(\{E\in\mathfrak{P}(O^{2})\setminus\emptyset\mid(e,e)\in E\})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{u\#\overline{u^{\prime}}\mid(u,u^{\prime})\in P,\exists v\in\Sigma^{*}:uv\in\Sigma^{\triangle},u^{\prime}v\in\Sigma^{\triangle},\psi(\ext_{u,v})=\psi(\ext_{u^{\prime},v})=e\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{u\#\overline{u^{\prime}}\mid u,u^{\prime}\in\Sigma^{*},\exists v\in\Sigma^{*}:uv\in\Sigma^{\triangle},u^{\prime}v\in\Sigma^{\triangle},\psi(\ext_{u,v})=\psi(\ext_{u^{\prime},v})=e\}\,\,\text{.}

For all
w\in\Sigma^{\prime\triangle}
we have

\varphi^{\prime}(w)=\begin{cases}1&\text{if $w=\varepsilon$}\\
\varphi(w)&\text{if $w\in\Sigma^{\triangle}\setminus\{\varepsilon\}$}\\
\overline{\varphi(w^{\prime})}&\text{if $w=\overline{w^{\prime}}$ for $w^{\prime}\in\Sigma^{\triangle}\setminus\{\varepsilon\}$}\\
\{(\psi(\ext_{u,v}),\psi(\ext_{u^{\prime},v}))\mid v\in\Sigma^{*},uv,u^{\prime}v\in\Sigma^{\triangle}\}&\text{if $w=u\#\overline{u^{\prime}}$ for $(u,u^{\prime})\in P$}\\
\bot&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

We prove it by structural induction on
w
. The cases when
w=\varepsilon

or
w=c\in\Sigma_{\text{int}}
or

w=\overline{c}\in\overline{\Sigma}_{\text{int}}
follow immediately from the
definition of
\varphi^{\prime}
.
In case
w=\#=\varepsilon\#\varepsilon
, we have

\varphi^{\prime}(w)=\{(\psi(\ext_{\varepsilon,v}),\psi(\ext_{\varepsilon,v}))\mid v\in\Sigma^{\triangle}\}=\{(\psi(\ext_{\varepsilon,v}),\psi(\ext_{\varepsilon,v}))\mid v\in\Sigma^{*},\varepsilon v\in\Sigma^{\triangle}\}

as required.

For the inductive step first assume

w=\alpha w^{\prime}\beta
for
w^{\prime}\in\Sigma^{\prime\triangle}
,

\alpha\in\Sigma_{\text{call}}\cup\overline{\Sigma}_{\text{call}}
and

\beta\in\Sigma_{\text{ret}}\cup\overline{\Sigma}_{\text{ret}}
.
If
w^{\prime}
is neither in
\Sigma^{\triangle}\cup\overline{\Sigma}^{\triangle}

nor of the form
u\#\overline{u^{\prime}}
with
(u,u^{\prime})\in P
, then

\varphi^{\prime}(w^{\prime})=\bot
by induction hypothesis and thus

\varphi^{\prime}(w)=\psi^{\prime}(\ext_{\alpha,\beta})(\varphi^{\prime}(w^{\prime}))=\psi^{\prime}(\ext_{\alpha,\beta})(\bot)=\bot

as required, since
w
is also neither in

\Sigma^{\triangle}\cup\overline{\Sigma}^{\triangle}
nor of the form

u\#\overline{u^{\prime}}
with
(u,u^{\prime})\in P
.
If
w^{\prime}=\varepsilon
, then
\varphi^{\prime}(w^{\prime})=1
and hence

\displaystyle\varphi^{\prime}(w)

\displaystyle=\psi^{\prime}(\ext_{\alpha,\beta})(\varphi^{\prime}(w^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=\psi^{\prime}(\ext_{\alpha,\beta})(1)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\psi(\ext_{\alpha,\beta})(1_{R})=\varphi(w)&\text{if $\alpha\in\Sigma_{\text{call}}$ and
$\beta\in\Sigma_{\text{ret}}$}\\
\overline{\psi(\ext_{a,b})(1_{R})}=\overline{\varphi(ab)}&\text{if $\alpha=\overline{b}\in\overline{\Sigma}_{\text{call}}$ and
$\beta=\overline{a}\in\overline{\Sigma}_{\text{ret}}$}\\
\bot&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

as required, because
w=\overline{b}\,\overline{a}=\overline{ab}
in
the second case.
If
w^{\prime}\in\Sigma^{\triangle}\setminus\{\varepsilon\}
, then

\varphi^{\prime}(w^{\prime})=\varphi(w^{\prime})\in R
by induction hypothesis, and hence

\displaystyle\varphi^{\prime}(w)=\psi^{\prime}(\ext_{\alpha,\beta})(\varphi^{\prime}(w^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=\psi^{\prime}(\ext_{\alpha,\beta})(\varphi(w^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\psi(\ext_{\alpha,\beta})(\varphi(w^{\prime}))=\varphi(w)&\text{if $\alpha\in\Sigma_{\text{call}}$ and
$\beta\in\Sigma_{\text{ret}}$}\\
\bot&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

as required.
If
w^{\prime}\in\overline{\Sigma}^{\triangle}\setminus\{\varepsilon\}
, then
